#192. Robot vessels take on the dangerous work of mine hunting

This South Korean vessel is among the ships damaged in the Strait of Hormuz

Traversing the Strait of Hormuz remains one of the most dangerous trips a vessel can take.

Despite assistance from the US Navy, ships are still being attacked.

"To transit the Strait of Hormuz you need agreement between the captain and the crew to take the risk. You need war risk insurance. And the captain has to make the final decision," says Steve Cameron, a former merchant navy officer and maritime consultant.

Missiles and drones are a threat from the air, but under the surface mines may also be lurking.

"If you're a captain, you'd like to see evidence mines aren't present," Cameron adds.

Modern mines rest on the seabed and detonate when they detect an acoustic or magnetic signal, triggering a pressure wave that will break the keel of a passing vessel.

Others can rise through shallow waters or float, or be powered towards a target.

The technology itself is not difficult or costly, but the mere threat can stop a tanker fleet from sailing.

Ian McFarlane of French defence group Thales is a naval architect who knows mine warfare well. "Mines are a very cheap way of waging a naval war, as the Royal Navy is acutely aware," he says.

Finding sea mines has traditionally been the job of specialist mine-hunting vessels, with crews of around 80 sailors.

To keep humans out of harm's way, an increasing amount of the work is done by systems that are autonomous and monitored from the shore.

They might only need 20 personnel, working in shifts on land. With the recruitment and retention challenges facing Nato navies these highly-portable robot systems are very attractive.

Such tech has been under development for 20 years.

During the 2003 Iraq war the US Navy poked around the seabed looking for mines with a torpedo-shaped robot called the Remus 100.

Pinging around with sonar these little machines operated down to around 100ft depth and spared divers a difficult and dangerous task.

Iran's military published this picture of a US submersible captured in the Gulf

The latest conflict in the Gulf is testing much more capable systems.

The US military is sparing with details about their mine-hunting craft, but we have seen glimpses of them.

Iranian forces hauled an uncrewed US autonomous sub, external out of the waters of the Strait of Hormuz in early September.

The Pentagon claimed this robot, an Extra Large Uncrewed Underwater Vehicle (XLUUV) from US autonomous defence tech venture Anduril, was a malfunctioning older device with no classified equipment on-board.

Whatever its mission, no sailors were imperilled. That, says William Lupton, a naval analyst with Shephard Group, "was a drop in the ocean compared to having prisoners taken", and part of the point of uncrewed vessels.

What if an uncrewed vessel is captured? Nato navies are reluctant to go into details, but computer drives can be wiped or destroyed remotely by various means. This may have taken place inside the humming circuit boards of the XLUUV seized by Iran.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle is packed with sensors

Such autonomous tech is being embraced by forces around the world.

Cooperation between Thales, the Royal Navy, and France's Marine Nationale has produced a robot motor launch.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle (USV) is packed with sensors programmed to detect mines via sonar that was developed for the oil and gas industries.

It tows a 5m long rectangular box, similar to a large torpedo, that takes a hi-resolution look at the seabed and creates a 3D picture refined by AI. The latest mines are designed to blend into their surroundings and the software spots anything that looks like a mine.

Autonomous systems are cheaper than conventional minehunters, plus they can be deployed, along with containerised control centres, by air.

Steve Prest, associate fellow at the RUSI defence think tank, introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy and was its first fleet robotics officer, a title he acquired in 2014.

"There is a bunch of ways to look for a mine using robot boats or robot subs, and while you can't prove the negative, that no mines are there, you can reduce the risk."

He says US robot minehunters can either blow up a mine or just mark the spot as somewhere to avoid.

"We'll see more use of autonomy but also the missions and capability will expand. And AI will be analysing data from those vessels on the spot as opposed to data being analysed when a drone returns."

The Royal Navy recently announced its concept of a hybrid navy - mixing drones with conventional warships. But Prest thinks for this vision to succeed the fleet must operate as one distributed force, sharing data in what he terms a cognitive architecture.

The robot only makes a real difference as part of this scheme and it's a mistake to focus on individual uncrewed assets. "Uncrewed or autonomous vessels are not the operating system. They are applications running on it."

Steve Prest introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy

While robots are on the rise, people are what really matter in the Strait.

Neil Roberts speaks for insurers at Lloyd's of London on marine matters and says the psychological impact of the mine threat is inescapable, as is the uncertainty around just how many mines, if any, are out there.

"The US says it has cleared the Omani route and believes the mine threat is being managed, with shipments running at about 75% of pre-conflict levels."

Fear of the unknown is the most potent aspect of the sea mine threat and robots are now the weapon of choice to confront that fear.

#188. How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How close is Donald Trump to Jensen Huang?

The Nvidia boss was one of the few business leaders with direct access to the US president. He had met Trump repeatedly, travelled with him to Britain and, in May, joined him on Air Force One for his trip to China.

Jensen Huang wasn't just another tech billionaire seeking the president's ear. He had become an important voice in one of the biggest arguments inside the administration: how much of America's most advanced technology should be allowed to reach China.

Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution. Washington has restricted exports of its most advanced processors to China since 2022, believing they could help Beijing develop its military and technological capabilities.

But enforcing those restrictions is difficult. Chips can be bought legally by customers in countries such as Singapore and Malaysia and then diverted to China. Chinese companies can also rent access to computing power through data centres outside the country.

Jensen Huang has long argued that the restrictions are counterproductive. They shut Nvidia out of a huge market and encourage Chinese companies to develop their own alternatives. Trump appeared sympathetic to this view, allowing Nvidia to resume sales of certain chips to China last year in return for a share of the revenue, although its most advanced Blackwell chips remain off limits.

But that relationship now looks rather more complicated.

The allegations surrounding Jensen Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, of no relation but a former executive at Singapore-based company Megaspeed, have raised the possibility that a foreign intelligence operation was targeting not just a powerful businessman, but someone with unusually close access to the American president.

US intelligence reportedly suspects Alice Huang of having links to Chinese intelligence. Beijing has rejected the allegations. Nvidia's board says it has no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised. But the affair has opened up a much bigger question: what did the administration in Washington know, and what did it do about it?

That question leads directly to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick.

His department was responsible for controlling exports of sensitive American technology. It was also being pressed to act on concerns about Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

On Thursday, Megaspeed chief executive, Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan, said the company "takes its compliance obligations under US export control laws extremely seriously" and is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government". He said the company had co-operated with US Commerce Department inspections.

A highly classified memo was shared with Lutnick about the concerns surrounding Alice Huang and Megaspeed. He resisted taking action against the company, a decision that is now under scrutiny.

Other senior officials wanted a tougher response.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was among those who reportedly questioned why Megaspeed had not been placed on the Commerce Department's blacklist.

On Friday, Bessent pointed the finger at Commerce for not blacklisting the company, telling CNBC that several government agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

There have also been questions about why an investigation into the Singapore-based company was stopped after Nvidia intervened. The New York Times reported that Nvidia's general counsel contacted Commerce Department investigators probing suspected chip smuggling by Megaspeed invoking a senior Trump appointee. The department allegedly then froze its investigation and handed the case to the Justice Department.

This apparently highlighted a significant disagreement about the direction of Trump's China policy. Huang wanted access to the Chinese market. Other officials were supposedly worried about the national security consequences. Lutnick was caught in the middle.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

Bessent blames Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to block Megaspeed

Members of Congress from both parties want answers. Democrats have launched an investigation into how the Commerce Department vetted Chinese companies approved to buy AI chips. Republicans are also questioning the administration's approach.

And Jensen Huang's departure has not eased the pressure.

Republican representative John Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said it was in the national interest for Nvidia and other American AI firms to succeed, but that companies "should never jeopardise our nation's security" by giving Chinese firms access to their most advanced chips.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said Huang's resignation did not absolve him of responsibility, and urged Lutnick to explain why export control loopholes remain open.

Meanwhile Jensen Huang's team told Republican lawmakers on Wednesday that he plans to cooperate with their request to testify behind closed doors.

The White House has not responded to the BBC's request for comment.

It has, however, told the New York Post that Trump has complete confidence in Lutnick, describing him as "the most transformative commerce secretary in modern history".

That is a fairly emphatic show of support, particularly amid reports that officials are weighing whether Lutnick should be forced out.

There are some obvious questions. If Lutnick had indeed been warned about the risks, why did his department fail to act? And if other senior officials were indeed pressing for action, why did it take so long?

The BBC has contacted the Commerce Department for comment. But there is an even bigger question for Trump.

The president cultivated a close relationship with Huang. He was directly involved in decisions about chip sales to China. And, according to reporting in the New York Times, he was briefed on the intelligence concerns.

This is becoming difficult to dismiss as the misjudgement of one businessman or one government department.

Jensen Huang's personal life may have triggered the crisis, but the questions now reach into the White House itself. How much influence did he have? What did the president know? And who was ultimately responsible for protecting America's most sensitive technology?

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

#186. Ex-GM CEO on Mary Barra succession planning: 'It's going to be tough'

Former General Motors CEO and Chairman Dan Akerson doesn't mince words when asked about the challenges the automaker faces in finding a successor for GM CEO Mary Barra: "It's going to be tough."

He should know. The 78-year-old businessman and U.S. Navy veteran helped with his own succession planning and recommended to GM's board in 2013 that Barra replace him as CEO. She has held the top job since Jan. 15, 2014.

Akerson said Barra will be hard to replace, citing her success and prominence as the first female CEO of any major automaker and the ongoing challenges facing the automotive industry, such as artificial intelligence, vehicle digitalization, and the rise of China and autonomous vehicles.

"It's going to be very, very dynamic," Akerson told CNBC during a phone interview. "It's going to be a big change."

Akerson declined to discuss potential internal candidates such as GM Chief Product Officer Sterling Anderson, CFO Paul Jacobson or President Mark Reuss, but he said the eventual successor needs to be dynamic and, if from the outside, "adaptable."

His comments to CNBC follow a Wall Street Journal report on Tuesday that GM is working with an executive search firm to identify possible outside candidates to succeed Barra, in addition to internal prospects. GM declined to comment on the report or Barra's succession.

Akerson, who came to GM as an outsider from The Carlyle Group in 2010, said selecting an outside candidate might be risky if there are already executives within the organization who have similar leadership and technical expertise.

"Is there anybody in the organization that has that background that you know better day to day over a period of time? You may be taking a risk by bringing in an outsider," he said.

The prospect of an outsider leading GM would be unusual in the auto industry, where major automakers have generally promoted internal CEO candidates, apart from periods of exceptional disruption such as the Great Recession.

Stellantis, BMW, Toyota Motor, Nissan Motor and Hyundai Motor have all appointed internal executives to their CEO positions in recent years.

Barra has said several times that her successor and her longevity in the role would be determined by the board.

A notable influence is likely Hewlett Packard veteran Patricia Russo, GM's longest-serving board member who has been on the board since 2009 and leads its CEO succession planning as independent lead director.

GM's CEO succession planning is done "at least annually," according to the company's yearly proxy filing. That process includes Barra providing the board "with recommendations for, and evaluations of, potential CEO successors and discusses developmental plans for these successors," according to the filing.

That is followed by board members engaging "with CEO candidates and senior management talent at Board and committee meetings and other forums to enable directors to personally assess candidates."

When Akerson was preparing to retire, Barra was among a pool of four candidates — two longtime GM employees and two relative newcomers — who were finalists for CEO in 2013, he said.

Akerson said he put the finalists through an extended evaluation, asking how they viewed GM's strengths and weaknesses, how they would organize the company, and their willingness to work together, potentially with other CEO candidates who didn't get the job.

Akerson said the final decision to appoint Barra was done at a day-and-a-half-long board meeting in Washington. The candidate field was narrowed to Barra and former GM executive Dan Ammann, an ex-Wall Street banker who was GM's CFO before leaving the automaker's Cruise unit, reportedly over a disagreement in strategy with Barra, in 2021.

"Mary's very deserving of all the accolades she's received," Akerson said. "She's done a terrific job."

Barra has been leading the automaker for nearly 13 years, which makes her the longest-serving CEO of the automaker since Alfred Sloan retired after a 23-year career as CEO in 1946.

Akerson said he believes that, like he did, Barra will have major influence in picking her successor. He described that dynamic as integral to continuing her legacy leading the automaker.

"This is important. I think if she doesn't pick the right person, in four or five years from now and things are back in the trough, that will tarnish her history. But I think she'll pick the right one," he said.

#184. Europe plans to catch the next computer chip wave

Photonic chips use light instead of electrons to process data

"Photonic chips are on the verge of a breakthrough," says Ton van Mol of the Dutch research institute TNO.

If van Mol is right â and TNO, the European Union and the Dutch government are backing the idea with millions of euros - then the technology could be a gamechanger.

The chips which run today's society, from your phone and TV, to the vast data centres meeting our insatiable desire for AI, are mostly electrical.

They process data by shunting electrons around tiny circuits â a single microchip produced today can contain billions of transistors, and sizes are measured in nanometres (one nanometre is a billionth of a metre).

Because they use light instead of electricity, photonic chips are faster, use less energy and generate less heat than traditional computer chips.

Van Mol hopes for an acceleration in photonics similar to the one seen in electrical chips since the 1970s.

"With optical chips, you are now where electronic chips were maybe 40 [or] 50 years ago."

American, Taiwanese and Chinese firms dominate the computer chip industry, but Europe sees the emergence of photonic chips as a fresh opportunity to lead, on what could be, a key future technology.

Which is why the European Union has committed â¬400m ($450m; Â£330m) to a project called PIX Europe, external, with the stated goal of making the continent "a global leader in emerging technology for Photonic Integrated Chips".

"Europe said 'we need to have our own manufacturing in-house'," says van Mol, who is leading a key part of the programme.

"We cannot depend like in the past that everything is manufactured in the US or China - that's not wise, so the [European] Chips Act was called into life, and one of the parts of the act is to build pilot lines to really bring the technology, which is now in labs, to the next stage - manufacturing."

Electronic and photonic chips are produced on wafers made up of hundreds of individual chips. Factories with machines that can handle bigger wafers can churn out chips more efficiently.

"[Photonic chip] manufacturing worldwide is [currently] done on wafers which are four inches (10cm) in size, and the next step is to scale-up production to a six-inch wafer," says van Mol.

His team are already building a 10,000 sq m factory to produce six-inch indium phosphide wafers on the High Tech Campus in the Dutch city of Eindhoven.

It's due to fully open in 2030 and will be a major step forward, not just for photonics in Europe, but around the world, according to van Mol.

"That's the development TNO is going to do, in a pilot line which is really serious.

"In 2030, we hope that we have a stable, reproducible, high-yield process, and then the intention is that the pilot line [opens] to industrial parties who can make commercial production out of it."

Netherland's based ASML is a crucial supplier of chip-making equipment

Europe does have one crucial player in the current computer chip industry - ASML.

The Netherlands-based tech giant is the only company in the world able to build the machines needed to make the most sophisticated semiconductors.

Which is why ASML products will sit at the heart of the pilot line in Eindhoven - it's no coincidence that van Mol's project is based around the corner from ASML's headquarters.

"The way you produce these [photonic] chips is very similar to the way you produce electrical or electronic chip," says van Mol.

"You use the same type of equipment, you also need a high-end clean room, you also use wafers, so the production method is very similar to the traditional semiconductor technology."

ASML itself is keen to explore the opportunities that the emerging technology of photonics could offer.

"Europe has a strong position in photonics research. The next challenge is translating those innovations into industrial-scale manufacturing," says Stanislas Baron, who is the head of ASML's EU/US Customer Team.

Peter Wennik says TNO's pilot production line is a "launchpad" for European tech

ASML's unique success has helped build an influential and lucrative network of companies and institutions devoted to semiconductor technology in and around Eindhoven, and it's a model for what Europe is trying to achieve with photonic chips.

The Eindhoven project has the backing of former ASML chief executive Peter Wennink, author of an influential report for the Dutch government called The Roadmap to Future Prosperity, external, which noted that the market for photonics was expected to grow 10 to 20 times in the next 10 years.

In a news release backing the new pilot line, Wennink described it as "a launchpad for Europe's future digital economy".

"By investing in scalable production capacity, we are creating an ecosystem that can help shape the global technological direction," he said.

Of course, Europe is not alone in looking to the potential of photonics to earn a global economic advantage.

In June this year, US photonics company Coherent announced what it called "the world's first and largest volume-production six-inch indium phosphide manufacturing platform" at its plant in Sherman, in Texas.

The expansion is backed by chip manufacturing giant Nvidia, external, as well as $50 million public funds under the US Chips Act.

It's an illustration of how quickly the market is moving, and how central photonic chips could be to the continuing race to retain an influential stake in AI and technological development more generally.

Europe's bid to establish itself as a global leader in photonic chips â exemplified by the Eindhoven project â is the kind of strategy that makes sense in a globalised economy, says Joris Teer, policy analyst at the European Institute for Security Studies in Brussels.

If the continent can take a lead in photonic chips, then future developments in AI and data will partly be dependent on Europe.

"Europe knows that its high energy and labour costs, skilled workforce shortages, and lack of private and public capital make an across-the-board reindustrialisation strategy impossible", Teer says.

"To retain influence, ensuring dependence on European parties rather than European autonomy is key."

Once up and running, the Eindhoven project will provide a pilot production line which can be used multiple companies.

It will be open to businesses across Europe and beyond, who can design and build chips without having to develop their own expensive production facilities.

"What we want to establish here is what we call a foundry model; there's no intention to have our own product," Ton van Mol says.

Instead, they will produce to order for outside customers.

It's the same idea which, at huge scale, has brought Taiwanese-based giant TSMC a position of global dominance in the manufacture of microchips.

Photonic chips are already in use, in highly specialised areas in data centres and in Lidar (light detection and ranging) sensor technology, for instance.

For van Mol of TNO, helping the industry to scale up, by easing production challenges and increasing capacity, is the key step to moving them into the tech we use in our daily lives, like phones and laptops, and to drive innovation in areas like medical scanners and 6G communications.

"The demand is there", says van Mol, "and the end goal is to have, in Europe, manufacturing of these chips, which is strategically very important because these chips [could] become the backbone of all data and telecom."

#182. Hedge funds are no longer just for the rich. They’re wooing everyday investors

For decades, hedge funds were the preserve of billionaires, pension funds and deep-pocketed institutional investors. Now, the industry is increasingly courting a new pool of capital: everyday investors.

The shift, dubbed by some as the "retailization" of hedge funds, comes as alternative asset managers seek new avenues for growth and investors look for ways to shield their portfolios from increasingly volatile equity markets.

Unlike traditional equity funds, which typically rely on stocks rising to generate returns, hedge funds can employ strategies including short selling, arbitrage and derivatives to seek profits across different market conditions.

For an industry already managing record levels of assets, individual investors represent a largely untapped growth opportunity, experts who spoke to CNBC said.

"Hedge funds are entrepreneurs. Their objective is to make as much money as they possibly can, both in returns and for their organization," said Don Steinbrugge, CEO of hedge fund consulting firm Agecroft Partners.

"So if they have the ability to go after a new market and grow their business, they're going to do that."

The push is coming from both hedge fund managers seeking to broaden their investor base and traditional asset managers looking to capitalize on growing demand for alternative investments, said Ken Heinz, president of hedge fund research firm HFR.

"Heightened geopolitical uncertainty, greater market volatility, inflation concerns, and the higher correlation between equities and bonds have raised questions about the effectiveness of conventional portfolio diversification," said Ricky Siao, a hedge fund specialist at Swiss private bank UBP.

The investment environment has become more challenging for investors who have historically relied on traditional allocations to equities and fixed income, he added.

As a result, investors are increasingly seeking exposure to strategies such as arbitrage, event-driven investing and quantitative macro strategies to reduce their dependence on equity market returns.

The democratization of hedge fund investing is taking shape through a growing array of investment vehicles, often with lower fees, smaller minimum investments and more frequent opportunities to withdraw money.

Some of the biggest names in the hedge fund industry are already moving in that direction.

Billionaire investor Bill Ackman's Pershing Square raised $5 billion in April for a publicly listed closed-end fund, giving everyday investors access to its investment expertise without the performance fees traditionally associated with hedge funds. The fund charges a 2% annual management fee.

British hedge fund giant Man Group, meanwhile, entered the U.S. ETF market last September and doubled its lineup to four actively managed funds just three months later.

The broader shift is also changing how hedge fund strategies are packaged for individual investors.

"What has changed most is the wrapper, not the strategy itself," said Yi Ling Ong, managing partner and chief operating officer at Golden Horse Fund Management.

In other words, hedge fund strategies are increasingly being packaged in more accessible vehicles such as mutual funds and ETFs, often with lower investment minimums and fewer restrictions on withdrawals.

"Investors may still be getting exposure to long/short equity, credit, or macro strategies, but delivered through vehicles that offer quarterly or monthly liquidity and standardized reporting."

In Europe, hedge fund managers have adapted strategies to fit regulated UCITS funds, which are accessible to individual investors. In the U.S., mutual funds and ETFs have similarly broadened access to alternative investment strategies.

In Asia, digital wealth platforms are helping bridge the gap between institutional and individual investors.

Singapore-based Endowus, which began offering hedge funds on its platform in 2022, has lowered minimum investments for semi-liquid funds to $50,000, while removing minimums altogether for some daily-liquid alternative funds, according to Chief Investment Officer Hugh Chung.

While most hedge funds on the platform remain restricted to accredited investors, a growing number of alternative funds are becoming available to non-accredited investors, Chung said.

India is seeing a similar shift through Specialized Investment Funds, or SIFs, which allow investors to access more sophisticated strategies through structures that more closely resemble traditional mutual funds, said Rajesh Singla of Alpha AMC.

Assets under management in India's Specialized Investment Funds have more than quadrupled since March to 426.7 billion rupees as of end-October, according to data from the Association of Mutual Funds in India.

The products carry a minimum investment of 1 million rupees, significantly below the entry thresholds typically associated with traditional hedge funds.

The shift is also making hedge fund-style investing cheaper.

Traditional hedge funds have long been associated with the "2 and 20" fee model, which typically charges a 2% annual management fee and taking 20% of investment profits.

While those fees have come under pressure over the years, retail-friendly alternatives such as hedge fund-like ETFs typically charge a single annual management fee of between 0.4% and 1.25%, according to Ong.

But lower fees does not necessarily mean better outcomes.

Retail investment vehicles often face tighter regulatory and liquidity requirements, which can limit managers' ability to replicate strategies used by traditional hedge funds.

Steinbrugge said those constraints can force managers to avoid less liquid securities that may offer some of the greatest opportunities for outsized returns.

"What investors are really choosing between is not just price, but structure," Ong said.

The industry's expansion into the retail market is also raising questions about whether individual investors fully understand the complexity of the strategies they are buying into.

Easier access does not eliminate the risks associated with leverage, short selling or illiquid investments.

"The key risk is that investors may underestimate the complexity and risk associated with these strategies simply because they are now more accessible," Singla said.

Liquidity is another concern, particularly for less liquid alternative investments being marketed through retail-friendly structures.

Some interval funds, which are increasingly being used to distribute private credit and other alternative investments to individuals, may offer to repurchase only 5% of their outstanding shares each quarter, Steinbrugge said.

That could leave investors unable to withdraw as much money as they want during periods of market stress.

Still, industry watchers largely see the rise of retail-friendly products as complementary to traditional hedge funds rather than an existential threat to them.

While the proliferation of more liquid, transparent and lower-fee products could intensify competition and pressure traditional fee structures, Siao does not expect retailization to fundamentally reshape the industry in the foreseeable future.

"I would expect this to be an incremental evolution rather than a fundamental transformation of the hedge fund business model," he said.

"Over time, the industry will likely remain segmented," Ong said. "Retail vehicles will address accessibility and scale, while hedge funds will continue to focus on depth, flexibility, and outcomes that are difficult to replicate in simplified formats."

#180. Should AI be judging our credit worthiness?

Sophie Hope's credit score was hit by identity fraud

Getting approval for a mortgage, a car loan or even a mobile phone contract can rest on a credit score.

Sophie Hope was on the sharp end of just such a decision.

"When I first checked my credit score, it was excellent. But then my mortgage application was suddenly denied by the lender because of my credit report. It was a massive shock as I'd never had bad credit. When I looked into it, my score had gone to 'very poor'," she says.

It turns out, the Brighton-based nail technician and content creator, had been the victim of identity fraud.

"Someone had opened accounts in my name across various catalogue sites and ordered multiple items, including a PlayStation 4 and Nike socks," she says.

"Without me knowing, the debt was transferred to a secondary finance company. It spent three to four years accumulating in the background. It was only when I went to apply for a mortgage that I discovered how severely it had dragged down my credit score," she says.

Credit scores are "backward looking" says Funmi Olufunwa

Hope's experience shows how crucial a credit score can be, but, as in her case, it does not tell the full story.

"As a nation, we are overly obsessed with credit scores," says Funmi Olufunwa, a consumer finance lawyer and founder of financial education business, Hoops Finance.

Three firms - Experian, Equifax and TransUnion - produce credit scores in the UK, and they all work from roughly the same data.

"A credit score is backward looking. It's looking at what has happened in the past, which doesn't necessarily predict the future," says Olufunwa.

"But when somebody's looking to lend you money, they're looking at what actually is affordable," she says.

Traditional credit scoring systems also disadvantage "thin-file applicants" - young people and recent immigrants to the UK who won't have built up a credit history.

But with more financial data available than ever before - and a growing appetite for AI to make sense of it - could artificial intelligence help lenders work out who is safe to lend to?

In the US lending decisions will often hinge on a credit score from a company called FICO, which uses data from the big three credit assessment firms.

But AI-powered lending firms, Upstart and Zest AI have both built businesses which offer lenders an extra option when it comes to assessing a client's credit worthiness.

"Thanks to our machine-learning models that look at more than 2,500 variables, we believe we can make significantly more loans at low prices to borrowers from every demographic group," Upstart said in statement.

Mortgage expert and founder and CEO of The Mortgage Mum, Sarah Tucker says: "AI has the potential to help lenders analyse much broader sets of information, identify patterns more quickly and build a more individual picture of someone's affordability and risk."

If technology allows lenders to understand so-called thin-file applicants better, rather than simply applying increasingly rigid rules, Tucker believes there is "a real opportunity to improve financial inclusion".

But like with many other applications of AI into everyday life, there are concerns.

"Consumers need to understand what information is being used to make decisions about them, and they need to be able to challenge something when it is wrong," Tucker explains.

Credit scores have been a contentious topic for decades for this very reason, says Sandra Wachter, professor of Technology and Regulation at the University of Oxford.

"We are not assessing if past decisions were right," Wachter argues, adding, AI does not know about existing social inequalities that impact decisions and could just "replicate what it has seen in the past".

While companies like Upstart and Zest AI see technology as a tool for financial inclusion, Wachter believes that real transformation requires accountability, as well as automation.

She stresses that firms must apply existing regulations and establish new guardrails to ensure that AI does not simply perpetuate the biases of the past.

"Not putting any safeguards in place will fortify, foster, and enhance discrimination."

"We've invested in creating objective, automated evaluations to identify bias, and we've incorporated those evaluations into our model training process so it evolves to become even more accurate and more fair over time," the company said in a statement.

Evangeline Atkinson (left) and Laura Mills co-founders of Noggin HQ

Evangeline Atkinson had a frustrating experience over her credit score.

"I was at university, paying rent, paying bills, as I had been doing for many years. I tried to get a Â£30-a-month phone contract, and they said no," she recalls.

"When I dug into it, it was because I was completely invisible to the system. No record could be found for me at any of the big three credit reference agencies."

That experience inspired her to try to change the system.

In 2021, Atkinson founded a credit reference agency with her best-friend from school, who had also experienced difficulties getting credit.

Their firm, Noggin HQ, was authorised by the FCA in June. Atkinson explains it was set up to help consumers access affordable credit by analysing their current account transactional data.

The platform helps lenders interpret thousands of individual data points about a person's spending habits.

Crucially, it does this without using AI, which Atkinson has also argued "carries the risk of inbuilt bias".

"You have to be very careful", she warns. "It's very sensitive data and it would be very easy for the models to be inferring various kind of protected characteristics."

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August

Perhaps conscious of change, the big three credit scoring firms have all started experimenting with AI.

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August 2026, letting users check their credit score inside ChatGPT. Equifax launched a platform called Equifax Amplify AI, which it says uses machine learning to sharpen credit assessment and fraud detection.

Meanwhile, in March TransUnion announced it was integrating Google's Gemini models "within its proprietary analytic data assets".

As for the future of credit scoring, companies are starting to look beyond traditional methods, according to consumer finance lawyer Funmi Olufunwa.

"They are beginning to realise that traditional credit scoring is inherently backward-looking, and that there are better predictors of the future out there."

Whether consumers will be happy with AI playing a part in working out their future credit ratings is another thing.

Any change might be led by the younger, tech-savvy generation.

"They feel comfortable with their data being used this way, simply because it's the world they've grown up in.

"I wonder if older cohorts, who may not use technology as much, will be quite as comfortable with it," says Olufunwa.

#177. San Francisco is enjoying an AI boom, but worries about a crash are growing

The city of San Francisco is the heart of the AI sector

Lauren Goldboss is busy. The owner of a small San Francisco dog-walking company, she has been hiring staff and buying vans.

Fuelling the growth is her new office dog-walking service Paw Perk, popular with some local AI start-ups.

Her business is an example of how the AI boom in San Francisco is benefiting other sectors.

The city saw an exodus of residents during the pandemic and its downtown was weakened by remote work, but it's now enjoying a rejuvenation, with AI being the major driver.

Lauren Goldboss walks dogs for AI workers

Leading AI firms OpenAI and Anthropic â which have been hiring top engineering talent, often on eye-popping salaries â are based here. Anthropic, the company behind the Claude chatbot, last week listed on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

Thousands of other AI start-ups have also sprung up, external in San Francisco.

Signs of the boom are evident in soaring rents and house prices, falling office vacancies, and buses and billboards plastered with cryptic AI adverts.

But what if there is a significant downward correction in AI share prices and a resulting big fall in investment in the industry? The International Monetary Fund last month became the latest organization to warn, external that this was a possibility.

Concerns have since continued due to the scandal surrounding Jensen Huang, the former boss of AI firm Nvidia, based south of San Francisco, causing some wider AI stock falls. However, Nvidia remains highly profitable, and Wednesday of this week it reported that its latest quarterly profits had doubled from a year earlier to $65bn (Â£49bn).

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Wealthy AI workers send San Francisco house prices soaring

Follow the money: Who pays for the AI boom now?

What are the thoughts of AI workers in the city, and should San Francisco â no stranger to tech's boom-and-bust cycles â be preparing for a possible crash?

One AI worker employed in the city is convinced that a crash is coming. "I am certain that the house of cards will come downâ¦ and I'm sure it will affect me," says the man who asked that we didn't use his name.

He questions whether leading US AI companies can generate enough revenues to justify their enormous spending commitments as cheaper, increasingly capable AI models emerge, especially from China.

But he isn't losing sleep over it. "Why worry about things I can't control?"

Another San Francisco-based AI worker, who also asked to remain anonymous, says that a significant market correction is "totally possible".

Yet he adds that most people don't think of it as the core risk. Instead, he says that the bigger concern in his circles, and the one he shares, is the existential risk that AI could pose to humanity.

"In some weird way, a market correction being the worst-case outcome feels great."

If there is an AI crash, San Francisco could be hit far more than Silicon Valley which lies 40 miles to its south.

That's because the AI boom is very much centred on the city itself rather than the wider San Francisco Bay Area, which includes Silicon Valley.

One study, external found that 60% of all venture capital invested in US AI firms from 2023 to 2025 went to the companies located in San Francisco, dwarfing the significantly smaller percentage given to companies in Silicon Valley.

But some factors suggest that the impact of a crash in San Francisco may be softer than some fear.

For all the investment they have attracted, AI companies are employing relatively few workers in the city compared to previous tech booms. So there may not be that many jobs to lose in a downturn.

"Hiring isn't necessarily on the scale as we would have thought with the amount of venture capital and new company formation," says Jeff Bellisario, executive director of the Bay Area Council Economic Institute, a business-affiliated think tank.

Ted Egan, the chief economist for the City and County of San Francisco, adds that "because of AI, it is a good time for tech again in the city, but we are not seeing tonnes of overall tech job growth".

Despite the expansion of the AI sector, the city remains about 35,000 jobs shy of its pre-pandemic peak, estimates Enrico Moretti, an economics professor at the University of California, Berkeley.

Instead, much of the spending by AI firms in the US is going towards data centres and computing power, relatively little of which are located in the Bay Area. San Francisco might be the brains of the AI boom, but it isn't the brawn.

Nonetheless, a significant correction could still be "very painful" for San Francisco, warns Moretti. Less AI hiring and potential layoffs would ripple through the local service sector, likely to affect jobs in everything from restaurants, to retail, to personal services like dog walking.

"It is the spillover on service jobs that would actually be the main effect," he says.

Office vacancies could also rise, reducing downtown foot traffic, while rents and house prices could weaken.

Huge data centres are being built across the US to power AI

But AI isn't the only force driving San Francisco's recovery. Healthcare and tourism â which the city has been working to encourage, including by improving downtown safety and cleanliness â are also growing,, external which may help cushion against a possible AI downturn.

"Tourism is not fully back, but the growth has been healthy," says Egan, adding that it is a sign of confidence in San Francisco.

All told, Egan doesn't expect an AI crash to fundamentally alter San Francisco's economic trajectory.

"The city's economy has not been screaming during this AI boom, so I wouldn't expect it to grind to a halt if there was a slowdown in AI spending," he says. "I think of it primarily as a hiccup that we should expect, rather than a major course correction for the city's economy."

As to whether and how San Francisco's city government should prepare, there are limits to what it can do, note Bellisario and Moretti. But encouraging growth in sectors beyond technology and maintaining financial reserves could help.

For Goldboss, the dog-walking company owner, her philosophy is to "build while it is here". If the next tech bust comes â "always a reality in San Francisco" â she will try to adapt to what the market needs. "It is just the risk you kind of have to take," she says.

#174. I pay £250 a month for someone else to do my life admin - it's worth it

Maxine Dillon says she needed help to get through her to-do list

Maxine Dillon, a busy 44-year-old mum of two, was struggling with her life admin â the never-ending to-do list of household and personal chores.

Everything from arranging for a handyman to fix something, to posting her clothing returns on Vinted, she wasn't getting around to it.

"I felt like all the little jobs that I didn't have time for were mounting up," says Maxine, who is self-employed helping businesses with their use of social media.

Then last year, Maxine, who lives in Mansfield in Nottinghamshire, discovered a new company called Life Laundry.

Launched in 2025 by two women based in Wakefield, Yorkshire, you can pay them to do all your admin jobs for you. Such companies are known as life personal assistants, and in recent years a growing number have sprung up on both sides of the Atlantic.

Maxine, who earns Â£32,000 a year, doesn't have a massive amount of disposable income, but she decided to start spending Â£250 a month for five hours' work and thinks it is worth it as it also helps reduce her "mental load".

The owners of Life Laundry, Alicia Papachristou and Jane Forbes, now look after a host of things for Maxine. Other jobs they take care of include arranging insurance, sending school emails, and managing online subscriptions.

Much of the work they can do remotely, but once a month they travel to Mansfield to do the jobs that require them to be physically present.

"If someone asks us what we do, we say we're life PAs [personal assistants], and we ease your overwhelm," says Forbes.

Amanda says she and her husband made a conscious decision to spend more quality time with their family

With such companies also increasingly available in the US, another busy working mother, Amanda Sims Nichols in Austin, Texas, has also employed a firm to look after her life admin.

Amanda, who is a business consultant, has three children with her attorney husband, and decided to start paying for help with life admin after moving house, away from her family support network.

She pays $2,250 (Â£1,750) a month for 25 hours, and says she relishes offloading regular chores including the weekly food shop and clothes washing.

"Money cannot buy you everything, but it can buy you back some of your time," says the 45-year-old.

The company she uses, Queen of To Do, was founded by Kate Ginsberg in 2011. She says that interest has greatly increased since the pandemic.

The business now has more than 50 active users. Meanwhile, Life Laundry has 25 customers.

Both companies say demand is growing for their services as people's lives become increasingly frantic.

Ginsberg says that the work they do for clients can be very diverse, with no task too big or too small.

Some of the larger tasks her team has faced over the years include flying from Austin to New York City to buy a desperately needed Christmas gift, and finding a new wedding location for a client with just three days' notice due to an incoming hurricane.

Staff of life admin companies will do most jobs around the house for a client

Jess Baker, a UK chartered psychologist, says the appeal of such providers is understandable.

"Using a life PA, if you can afford one, takes the pressure off having to keep hundreds of to-dos swimming around your head, or on lists you might forget to look at," she says.

"Otherwise, the cognitive load can lead to feelings of being overwhelmed, excess worry and exhaustion."

While paying for help with such tasks may seem a luxury, for some people support with life's everyday trials is a lifeline.

Anna Allington sought help after her husband died. "My husband passed away. I was 47," she says.

"And the same week he passed away, I got leukaemia. There was a lot to sort out and I was very, very unwell."

Anna, now 51, still suffers the after-effects of her cancer. "I can't function executively like I used to," she says.

She pays Â£1,200 a month for 40 hours of help from Cheshire-based company Vi-Va. Her personal assistant helps her respond to emails, manage her diary and make travel plans and bookings.

"We meet twice a week on Zoom," says Anna, who lives in the Cotswolds, so they can go through all her tasks.

Many of us would love for someone else to do our laundry

Many more women than men use these companies.

Queen of To Do says just 20% of its clients are male, while only one of Life Laundry's 25 clients is a man.

Some of that disparity is likely due to mothers still looking after the lion's share of household admin, as studies continue to show.

However, Life Laundry's Forbes wonders if some men are too proud to ask for assistance.

"Men are struggling as well, but I think they're less likely to ask for help."

#171. South Korea upgrades growth forecast, raises benchmark rates to highest in two years

South Korea's central bank on Thursday hiked benchmark lending rates to their highest in two years, as inflation continues to run above target while a booming economy allows it room to tighten policy.

The central bank raised rates by 25 basis points to 3.25% for its final meeting of the year, in line with expectations from economists, data on the Bank of Korea's website showed.

Inflation is expected to remain above its 2% target level for a considerable time, the BOK added in its statement, saying this was due to the pass-through of elevated cost pressures and as demand-side pressures also gradually strengthen, driven by stronger economic growth. Inflation in the country stood at 2.8% in October.

The BOK also upgraded its growth forecasts for 2026 to 3.9% for 2026 and 4.1% for 2027, up from the 3.3% and 2.9%, respectively, forecast in August.

South Korea's third-quarter GDP grew by 3.4% year on year, beating expectations, allowing the central bank room to raise rates.

"The projections of high growth rates for next year and beyond also reflect the significantly increasing weight of the IT sector as a growth engine for the Korean economy," BOK Governor Shin Hyun-song said at a press conference following the decision.

Operating profits in the semiconductor sector could reach 700 trillion won, Shin told reporters, according to local news agency Yonhap.

Shin also signaled that more rate hikes, although he did not give a fixed timeline.

"While the domestic economy is expected to continue its robust growth, inflation is forecast to remain above the target level for a considerable time ... The Board, therefore, sees it warranted to continue its policy response until there is confidence that inflation will converge stably to the target level."

Gareth Leather, senior Asia Economist at Capital Economics said there is reason to believe that the BOK's tightening cycle was drawing to a close.

The BOK has raised rates by 75 basis points in five months, as higher energy prices from the Iran war and the AI boom has pushed up inflation in the country.

Leather said that the country's strong growth, as well as a strengthening won could ease concerns over inflation.

While rapidly rising property prices could strengthen the case for a prolonged tightening cycle, Leather said that the BOK was "more likely to favour macroprudential measures over further rate hikes to address its financial stability concerns."

After the decision, the country's benchmark Kospi rose 1.88%.

#170. Why tiny cars are taking off across Europe

Selena Buse, 16, reverses her CitroÃ«n Ami out of a parking space and heads off past the school playground. Heads turn. The Ami looks pretty remarkable. Futurist or like-a-toaster depending on your point of view. But it's changed her life.

"I got it because I really needed it to get to school. And I use it to visit friends on the weekends. It's indispensable in rural areas like where I live," she says.

In France, you can drive a car like this from the age of 14 with a road safety certificate and eight hours training. In Italy too.

In Germany and Spain it's 15, in the UK 16, external. Because they are classed as motorised quadri-cycles. Four-wheel mopeds in other words.

Registrations have risen from about 13,000 in 2019 to around 25,000 last year in France.

In Europe, sales have gone from 30,000 to 60,000 in the past five years. And the Ami, launched in 2020, has really changed the image, with teenagers who now account for a quarter of all mini-car sales.

In Selena's very small CitroÃ«n, we are soon rolling along at the top speed of 45km/h (28mph). For other road users, no-licence cars like this can seem like mobile traffic calming.

"When I'm on faster roads and there's a lot of traffic the cars behind me sometimes honk their horns but I can't go any quicker!" Selena says.

Daniel LaprÃ¨s says the Aixam car was "the exact solution to our problem"

Another local Daniel LaprÃ¨s, who drives a micro-car made by French firm and market-leader Aixam, tells me he actually pulls over to let the cars behind pass.

He and his wife moved from Paris to this country village two years ago. Of Canadian nationality, he didn't swap his foreign licence for a French one in time and lost the right to drive. That didn't matter much when he was in Paris. Like many Parisians he didn't need a car. He used public transport. But that's not possible here.

"We tried living off of taxis and it was in the end a disaster. Then we heard about how you could drive without having to have a licence, and it was the exact solution to our problem. Cheap, inexpensive to operate, basically no administrative formalities, you get insurance."

However, LaprÃ¨s feels less safe in his small car. "They're little more than tin cans on wheels, right? I mean, you're not going to be driving fast, but someone else could be."

These cars are forbidden on motorways. But even on medium-fast roads, being considerably slower than the average vehicle can be a problem.

"You go on national highways at your risk and peril. I did it once and I will never, ever do it again. On those roads (speed limit 110km/h), everybody is passing you, including the biggest trucks. And they come at you very fast from the rear, so it's scary," LaprÃ¨s says.

Daniel is 77 so fits in with the long-established market for these cars: older people living in the countryside.

Traditionally the market also included quite a few people who'd lost their licences, often because of drink-driving offences. But the launch of the electric CitroÃ«n Ami in 2020 changed the customer profile and the product.

"The launch of the Ami was the beginning of the transformation of the sector, with more and more electric models coming on to the market. In 2026, more than 67% of new licence-free cars are electric," says Marie-Laure Nivot, market analyst at AAA Data.

The launch of the CitroÃ«n Ami in 2020 "transformed the sector" analysts say

This tendency was encouraged by stricter EU regulations on motorised two-wheelers that had been the vehicles of choice for European teenagers until then.

"It proved too costly for manufacturers to apply the new EU rules, so mopeds and small scooters have almost disappeared from the market since 2020 and licence-free cars have taken their place," Marie-Laure Nivot says.

Tiny car sales rose strongly till 2024 although they have flattened since in France with the withdrawal of subsidies for electric models.

Curiously, the arrival in this niche market of a giant competitor in the shape of Stellantis brand CitroÃ«n was actually good news for existing French manufacturers like Aixam and Ligier.

"We're specialised in this niche market and we're very good at what we do, but of course, we didn't have the communication strength of a multinational like Stellantis. They helped us to make people understand that it was possible in France to drive this product from 14-years-old, in Germany from 15-years-old. They gave a lot of visibility to our market," says Aixam sales and marketing director Tom Faget.

Aixam has invested â¬30m (Â£25m; $33m) in expanding its production capacity in France. And its sales are growing in other European countries, even as the French market slows.

France's Ligier says young people are choosing tiny cars over scooters

Meanwhile, the competition is producing increasingly sophisticated minicars - some costing as much as a small conventional car. At Ligier, marketing director Romain Illegems says the biggest change is in who's buying them.

"We've gone from mobility that was imposed on people to mobility that people choose. Before, these vehicles were something you used because you absolutely needed to get around, because there was no public transport, or because you didn't have a licence.

"Today, teenagers are choosing them instead of scooters or mopeds, because they're safer and offer better protection from the weather. Young people now account for 40% of our customers."

There are limitations, of course. Just two seats, a maximum speed of 45km/h and no motorways. But for Selena, who rents her Ami for â¬60 a month, plus insurance, it's a small price to pay for independence.

#166. US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

US Attorney General Todd Blanche has backed Nvidia as a "good corporate citizen" while refusing to comment on whether the AI chipmaker was under federal investigation.

Blanche said he had no information about newly reinstated CEO Jensen Huang's security clearance status, as lawmakers demand answers about US President Donald Trump's involvement in Huang's comeback.

Huang was given his job back less than two weeks after being forced to resign over revelations of extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

Asked several times on Tuesday whether the justice department or FBI had investigated Huang, Blanche responded: "I don't know what you're talking about."

Huang is still set to appear before Congress next week, where he will be questioned about national security concerns over his relationship with an alleged spy who previously ran Megaspeed, a Singapore-based corporate customer of Nvidia.

Trump has said he would consider pardoning Huang for any potential criminal activity, though he does not think it will come to that.

"It's always an option, I guess, but I don't think it'll be necessary," Trump told the New York Post in an interview published on Tuesday.

With Huang rescue, Trump riskily ties his political fortunes to Nvidia

The US commerce department has also announced it was preparing to blacklist the tech company Megaspeed, which has been suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

The commerce and justice departments said last week that they began investigating Megaspeed in 2024 "for potential export control violations".

But Blanche told reporters on Tuesday that the latter had "hundreds, if not more" export investigations occurring at any one time.

"So when you say the export investigation is still ongoing, I don't know what you're talking about," he said.

Asked if he was satisfied Nvidia had been co-operative with inquiries into whether it or Huang knew about chips being diverted to China, Blanche said: "I have no reason to stand up here and say Nvidia is anything but a good corporate citizen.

"Obviously, if Nvidia was the target or was subject to some criminal investigation or they were obstructing or thwarting our investigation, then that wouldn't be the message that the president is saying to the American people.

"It shouldn't be what my message would be either."

While Nvidia's stock market value surged on the announcement of Huang's reappointment, some of the gains were reversed in volatile Tuesday trading.

The company's share price closed down nearly 6% - still well above where it ended last week. On Monday, Nvidia stock rocketed more than 31% following news of Huang's return.

Huang reportedly apologised to Nvidia employees on Tuesday, the day after returning to the company as chief executive, saying he had made "terrible mistakes" and was now receiving daily 90-minute therapy sessions.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

The company's board reinstated Huang on Monday in a shock U-turn. Harvey Jones, its chairman, said Huang's return was in the "best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers".

The board said it had received input from senior levels of the US government and was confident that Huang's reinstatement posed no national security risk.

On Monday, Trump said he had spoken to the company's directors and convinced them to bring back Huang.

Huang's reinstatement provoked bipartisan anger.

Republican Congressman Don Bacon said Nvidia should have "ignored the administration's inexplicable desire to save Huang".

Republican Senator Pete Ricketts said Huang's behaviour "doesn't absolve Nvidia of answering for national security concerns and unaccounted-for chips".

Democratic Congressman Robert Garcia, the incoming chairman of the House Oversight Committee, said Trump's "direct involvement" with Nvidia's decision "raises serious national security and corruption questions".

Democratic Senator Ruben Gallego said Congress needed to "investigate what stock positions took place before the announcement".

#163. Beware higher correlation but nab high U.S. credit yields, fund managers say

The artificial intelligence boom could lead to a recession, whether through a collapse in AI investment or widespread job losses as the technology advances, a hedge fund manager said.

"I do worry that if we don't have an AI-led recession in a few years, we're going to have an unemployment-led recession," Sheru Chowdhry, founder and chief investment officer at DSC Meridian Capital, said during a panel discussion moderated by CNBC's Joanna Ossinger at the iConnections Global Alts Asia conference in Singapore last week.

Chowdhry's warning highlights a risk that extends beyond a potential bursting of the AI bubble. Rapid advances in the technology could lead to widespread job losses, with implications for the housing market and consumer spending, he said.

"If this continues to go at the pace it is, I think in a few years a lot of us will probably not have jobs," Chowdhry said, adding that investors would have to rethink their approach to sectors such as housing and discretionary spending.

Even so, the fund manager remains bullish on computing power and sees opportunities in data centers, despite concerns about AI valuations.

The current AI investment cycle reminds Chowdhry of the dot-com bubble and the telecommunications infrastructure buildout of the early 2000s.

"This cycle reminds me of the dot-com bubble and the telecom build we had going on in the early 2000s," he said. "Every cycle is different, but it kind of rhymes in some ways."

Chowdhry also questioned whether semiconductor companies could sustain their profit margins in the coming years.

However, he sees opportunities in data centers, pointing to access to power and grid connections as major bottlenecks in the AI buildout.

"If you can identify data centers, I feel like that's going to be the picks and shovels of this cycle," he said.

In particular, data centers that can secure permits and access to power within the next two years present a significant investment opportunity, he added.

Beyond AI infrastructure, Chowdhry also pointed to attractive yields in U.S. credit markets.

He cited yields of around 8.5% on U.S. high-yield bonds and 9.5% on leveraged loans, describing the current market environment as an opportunity to "get paid to wait."

Investment portfolios may be more vulnerable to market volatility than they appear, according to Dan Gelernter, director of AI at R.G. Niederhoffer Capital Management, who spoke on the same panel.

Average monthly correlations across investable assets rose from around 5% between the 1960s and 1990s to roughly 11% to 12% by 2020, according to research cited by Gelernter.

His own analysis suggests that the figure has climbed further to around 15% to 16% in 2026.

"Most portfolios are more highly levered, less liquid, more highly correlated than they appear to be, and that problem will get worse the more markets move," Gelernter said.

Within the AI trade, Joo Kian Ong, a partner at AP3X Capital, said Samsung Electronics would be a good long position, with SK Hynix or Micron on the other side as potential shorts.

#162. Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

As the market bell rang out at the Nasdaq today, the Nvidia super-bounce faded.

Its shares slid and are down almost 30% over the past six months, well before the scandal involving Jensen Huang emerged earlier this month.

The legitimate fears about the staying power of the tech boom pre-dated Huang's exit. They have not gone away with his return.

Investors are jittery. Already Anthropic is now trading notably lower than last week's stock market flotation. The firm is the first purely AI company to be publicly listed, and is being seen as a big test for continued investor appetite for the technology.

President Trump's extraordinary phone call to CNBC taking credit for telling the board to reinstate Huang showed the extent of the operation - of some in Silicon Valley and within the US administration - to get him back at the helm.

Among the truly astonishing reflections from President Trump was a suggestion that a two-day investigation of allegations was done and had cleared Huang of national security concerns.

The President said his remarkable move to intervene was because Huang is a "national treasure" who has the next generation of AI chips "in his head". Trump says this is why he needed to act.

In markets, those sceptical about the AI boom say they have never seen an operation to prop up a stock market boom like this. The bond vigilantes and short sellers are now up against Silicon Valley and the White House.

Investors are trading not revenue numbers but levels of political support at a time when US voters have just delivered a backlash against AI data centres.

Against this backdrop, the recovery of the central cash-generating foundation of the tech boom - Nvidia and its $400bn (Â£304bn) annual sales of advanced chips - looks politically fragile.

This worsens other uncertainties and risks, such as Nvidia's central role in the web of financing, borrowing and cross-ownership to support every corner of the build out of data centres.

The brief interim Nvidia chief executive Colette Kress had already signalled a rowing back from elements of this strategy last week. Where is that now?

The fall back in Anthropic's share price also raises questions about OpenAI's planned listing on the stock exchange. Ultimately these two companies might be the most critical part of all this.

They remain valuable companies with phenomenal technology relied on by the world's biggest companies. The real question is whether that reliance eases, as corporations eschew expensive frontier models for cheaper generic models offered by China.

In turn, all this could be affected by the political backlash, to which the Nvidia psychodrama now contributes.

It is simply not normal to have trillion dollar swings in the value of individual companies.

The extraordinary volatility shows the fragile mood of investors.

#161. Pizza chains are under pressure. GLP-1 use is only one part of the problem

Big pizza chains are struggling as weaker consumer spending, a wider variety of dining options and the rise of GLP-1 medications weigh on their sales.

The pandemic was a boom time for Domino's Pizza, Papa Johns, Little Caesars and Pizza Hut. But since dining rooms reopened, the biggest U.S. pizza chains have faced an uphill battle to retain their customers.

These days, consumers are watching their discretionary spending but have more delivery and takeout options when they do decide to order, thanks to the accessibility of third-party platforms. Plus, food quality and the customer experience have become more important to diners who are looking for value when they order from restaurants.

As a result of pizza fatigue and challenges facing the broader restaurant industry, the number of U.S. pizza restaurants has tumbled. Since pizza locations hit a peak of 83,035 nationwide in August 2019, more than 9,600 pizzerias had closed their doors as of July 2026, according to data from Datassential.

In 2025, sales at U.S. pizza restaurants fell 0.3%, according to Technomic data. Sales at independent pizzerias slipped 0.4%. Chains, which account for about two-thirds of the domestic pizza category sales, saw revenue shrink 0.3%.

Papa Johns reported that North American same-store sales declined 2.5% in 2025. Pizza Hut's U.S. same-store sales fell 5%. Among the three pizza chains that were publicly traded last year, only Domino's posted annual same-store sales growth at its U.S. restaurants, with an increase of 3%.

It isn't just diners who have lost their appetite for pizza. Since March 2020, Papa Johns stock has lost about two-thirds of its value. Even shares of Domino's, which dominates the category, have seen gains of only about 2% in that time. For comparison, the S&P 500 has climbed about 150% over the same period.

And Yum Brands sold Pizza Hut this year, offloading the laggard of its portfolio to LongRange Capital and Yum China after about two decades of stagnating sales.

"Pizza Hut has been a detractor for YUM ... Going forward, YUM won't be tripped up by pizza category dynamics (discounting, etc.), translating to stronger growth and higher concentration in higher growth industry segments and markets," Wells Fargo analyst Zachary Fadem wrote in a research note in September.

Pizza chains now have significantly more competition for diners' dollars than they did in 2020. Third-party delivery platforms, which grew in popularity during the pandemic, offer a wide variety of cuisines beyond the traditional pizza and Chinese. As a result, Domino's and Papa John's have to compete with other fast-food chains, like McDonald's and Chick-fil-A, for DoorDash and Uber Eats orders.

As pizza has grown more expensive, a pie seems less appealing to budget-conscious diners. The average large cheese pizza is now almost $17, according to the 2026 Pizza Power Report from PMQ Pizza, a trade publication.

Plus, frozen pizza is another convenient option, with a lower price tag. Retail sales of single- and multi-serve frozen pizza rose 2.9% in the 52 weeks ended Nov. 7 compared with the same period in 2022, according to NielsenIQ data.

"When the American consumer is focused on the least expensive eating occasion, the items that are in my pantry, my refrigerator and my freezer win every single time," said David Portalatin, a food and food service industry advisor for Circana.

Convenience stores like Casey's General Stores have also won over customers with pizza at a low price point.

"We believe that [Casey's] has an advantage over [quick-service restaurants] and dollar stores, which have fewer options to navigate the persistent inflationary environment, while [Casey's] can offset food pressures with margins," Bank of America Securities analyst Sara Senatore wrote in a research note published in June.

She added that geographic overlap of locations suggests that Casey's is stealing share from Pizza Hut.

The biggest pizza chains have also been slow to adapt to shifting consumer attitudes toward nutrition. For example, of the four biggest chains, Papa Johns is the only one to even test a pizza crust with added protein as more diners prioritize it in their diets. And the test was only in one Atlanta restaurant — for a single day.

Pizza's nutritional content more broadly is another drag for the category. Its carbohydrate and fat content, from its dough and cheese, respectively, can make it less appealing for consumers on GLP-1 drugs, which have skyrocketed in use over the last several years.

Spending on pizza quick-service restaurants has fallen 22.2% per GLP-1 household after six to eight months using the medications, according to a 2026 survey published by PwC.

To be sure, pizza's recent struggles do not mean an existential crisis for the category. Total pizza servings are down only about 1% this year, Circana's Portalatin said.

And chains are trying to reverse that downturn in servings. In August, Domino's launched the Domino, a customizable Detroit-style personal pizza to attract solo diners. In November, Papa Johns unveiled a new turnaround plan focusing on improving food consistency and restaurant operations. And Pizza Hut is running a holiday-themed meal deal.

"Pizza's not going anywhere," Portalatin said. "The American consumer loves pizza."
