#159. Inside India newsletter: Roaring economy, foreign investors’ return and now record auto sales

Hello, this is Priyanka Salve, writing to you from Singapore.

Welcome to the latest edition of "Inside India" — your one-stop destination for stories and developments from the world's fastest-growing large economy.

Despite 2026 being a challenging year with high global energy prices and supply chain disruptions, the Indian economy has fared surprisingly well. The auto industry, a key pillar of manufacturing and employment, has breached several records this year and is poised to report its best-ever sales.

This week, I spoke to experts to decode what this stellar performance means for one of the world's most promising consumer markets.

Any thoughts on today's newsletter? Share them with the team.

India's economy expanded at 7.8% in the June quarter, beating expectations, even as growth in several major economies cooled. Foreign portfolio investors who had been exiting Indian markets in droves for most of the year have also started returning in historic numbers.

And now, the world's third-largest auto market is set to for a record this year as better road connectivity, tax cuts, and easier financing options have fueled bumper sales over the last several months. That comes at a time when major auto markets across the world, from China to the U.S. have been seeing a slump in sales.

"10 years ago, despite having the money, people did not buy cars, but now even if they don't have the money, they take financing and buy," Amar Sheth, vice president of the Federation of Automobile Dealers Associations, told CNBC, pointing to the shift in consumer mindset.

They are becoming "more optimistic" about the future and, rather than waiting, consumers are willing to take a loan and purchase something useful to them now, he adds.

The industry body that Sheth represents said on Monday that automakers in India were poised to sell more than 30 million vehicles in the financial year ending March 2027, dubbing the sector as a "reflection of the wider India growth story."

From April to October, total vehicle retail sales in India reached 18.7 million units, up 10% from a year earlier, with car, tractor and commercial vehicle sales growing more than 15%, as per FADA data.

The optimism of the industry body is also shared by major automakers; several of whom have announced capacity expansion plans this year to meet the growing consumer demand.

Japanese automakers Maruti Suzuki and Toyota plan to raise capacity by 1.2 million units and 200,000 units, while Korea's Hyundai Motors will add capacity for 150,000 units, as per data shared by automotive industry intelligence provider Mobility Global. Leading Indian companies Tata Motors, Mahindra & Mahindra and JSW have also announced capacity addition plans, the data showed.

India is the only big auto market in the world that is growing while most others such as France, Germany, and Korea are "either declining or are flat," Puneet Gupta, director India and ASEAN at Mobility Global, told CNBC, explaining the reason behind car companies investing in the country.

Gupta said that the Chinese automotive market hit an inflection point in 2007, when the GDP per capita crossed $2,500, and now India is "at a similar point." He added that government support for the auto industry would be crucial in replicating the boom seen in the Chinese auto industry.

The catalyst for India's auto sector boom traces back to last September, when the government announced a wide range of tax cuts to spur domestic consumption.

The auto industry contributes 7.1% to the country's gross domestic product, 49% to manufacturing GDP, 15% to goods and services tax collection, and supports 30 million jobs, as per government data.

While the tailwinds from the tax cuts have faded, experts now believe that replacement demand and the growing reach of financing options to smaller cities will unlock the next phase of growth.

Consumption has been strong this year as the government has partly shielded people from rising energy costs while corporates, with their strong balance sheets, have been slow to pass on price hikes, Gaura Sengupta, chief economist at India's IDFC First Bank, said.

So far this year, wage growth has been good, the labor market has held up equity markets haven't corrected sharply, keeping the wealth effect intact, she said, underscoring the reasons driving consumption along with easy access to credit.

Despite the economy facing external shocks, producers are also "feeling confident to add capacity" as they have seen domestic demand sustain, Sengupta added.

However, experts said that the central bank's recent tightening of monetary policy and price hikes by automakers could slightly take the edge off the auto sales boom in the last few months of the financial year.

While urban demand is unlikely to get affected, rural sales growth could moderate, they added.

"Overall retail should close FY27 in high single digits," Diwakar Murugan, senior automotive analyst at Omdia, told CNBC, adding that this year's record sales are not a one-time spike but a "structural reset."

China has changed tactics but is still thwarting India's rise, a U.S. panel saysChina is "actively working to suppress India's rise," even as the relationship between the two major Asian powers has thawed in the past year, according to a U.S. panel that sees an opening for greater outreach to India.

U.K.-India trade deal faces a stiff test over Scotch whisky import taxesWithin months of taking effect, the India-U.K. trade deal is facing an early test, as New Delhi resists extending preferential tariffs to bulk Scotch whisky imports.

Investors give a thumbs down to Tata Steel-APL Apollo $1.67 billion deal, plunging sharesTata Steel, one of the world's leading steel producers, on Monday said it will buy a 26.4% stake in India's largest structural steel pipe and tube maker, APL Apollo, for 155.1 billion rupees ($1.67 billion). However, investors reacted negatively to the deal.

Nov. 27: Mahanadi Coalfields IPO listing.

Nov. 30: GDP data for July-September 2026.

#154. North Korea's Kim calls for nuclear arsenal expansion, says status 'will never change'

North Korean leader Kim Jong Un called for bolstering the country's nuclear arsenal and expanding the use of tactical nuclear weapons across multiple domains, state media reported Thursday, as Pyongyang reaffirmed its determination to retain its nuclear arms.

Kim's comments followed a working visit Wednesday to facilities involved in nuclear material production and weapons research, according to the Korean Central News Agency.

At the Nuclear Weapons Institute, he urged greater attention to "operationalizing" tactical nuclear weapons for a range of missions.

Kim directly linked the development of those weapons to North Korea "sharing a border with its most dangerous, hostile state," an apparent reference to South Korea.

The visit was part of Pyongyang's efforts to counter what Kim described as "the reckless expansion of adversary states' nuclear forces and military alliances," according to KCNA. He also singled out South Korea as a threat to North Korea's security and sovereignty.

Kim reiterated that North Korea's status as a nuclear-armed state was irreversible, dismissing "denuclearization rhetoric" and saying its nuclear status "will never change."

"[Our nuclear policy] is a core element of safeguarding the sovereignty of the Republic, enshrined in the supreme law of the state," he added.

The report did not identify the sites Kim visited or disclose any production figures.

The announcement followed an assessment last week by the International Atomic Energy Agency that another extension to Yongbyon's original uranium-enrichment facility had been externally completed by late October.

The nuclear watchdog has lacked access to North Korea since 2009 and monitors its activities using satellite imagery and open-source intelligence.

Citing what he portrayed as growing threats from Washington and its allies, Kim accused the U.S. of encouraging neighboring allies to acquire nuclear-powered submarines, pursuing nuclear sharing and the joint use of nuclear weapons.

His comments come after South Korea announced efforts to acquire nuclear-powered submarines after President Donald Trump backed the plan in October 2025, according to a White House joint fact sheet. Nuclear-powered propulsion does not itself mean that a vessel carries nuclear weapons.

Trump had expressed interest in meeting with Kim at the APEC summit in Shenzhen last week, according to Reuters, but no meeting took place and North Korea did not publicly respond to the proposal.

Trump denied that Kim had ignored him, adding that communications could "start up right away," in an interview with the New York Post Tuesday. He said the two leaders had not been in contact "recently."

"We've been off to other things," Trump added.

North Korea has continued to accelerate its nuclear weapons development and rebuff South Korean and U.S. attempts to resume dialogue since negotiations between Trump and Kim collapsed in 2019.

— CNBC's Jenny Lee contributed to this report.

#153. International first class is over for U.S. airlines. Enter the mega business-class cabin

The era of U.S. airlines' long-haul, international first class ended this week when American Airlines' last flight with its eight-suite section landed. In its place will be a massive business-class cabin with room for 70 passengers.

U.S. airlines have been ditching international first-class cabins for a new top tier: larger and souped-up business classes, featuring suites with sliding doors, the design du jour. Delta Air Lines and United Airlines have already taken this step. American was the last of the U.S. airlines to still have first class on long-haul international routes.

Many travelers might not notice the difference. That's because of airlines' investments into business class: new suites, bigger screens, better technology like device-charging pads, and improved bedding.

It's part of airlines' big bet on mass luxury. Consumers have been willing to shell out more to fly comfortably, which is helping to boost airline revenue. That's all the more important as costs of everything from fuel to airport landing fees have been rising, soaking up more of the industry's profits.

The retirement of first class ends a more-than-six-decade stretch of the jet age that in the early days included service like seat-side carving of chateaubriand and meals served on fine bone china.

A few decades ago, business class was much smaller, with two-by-two seats in some configurations, and recliners, not the lie-flat seats that dominate those cabins now.

"When you've got individual pods in biz class, and you've got a comfortable enough seat and bedding and decent wine, for a lot of people this is good enough," said Henry Harteveldt, founder of travel consulting firm Atmosphere Research Group and a former airline executive.

The over-the-top dining of the early days of international first class was a form of entertainment, long before laptops, personal seat-back screens and in-flight Wi-Fi, or even the promise of sleep on lie-flat beds, but customers still eat lots on board, Harteveldt said.

"People say they want to sleep but then they stay up and they eat every morsel," he said. "They're hypocrites."

There was still a hefty price gap between first and business.

A flight between Los Angeles and London on American's Boeing 777-300ER this week went for $12,994 in business class for a one-way ticket and $15,669 for a first-class seat.

American's last flight with international first class departed Friday morning local time in Sydney, bound for Los Angeles.

Its new cabin on its Boeing 777-300ERs will feature 70 business-class suites, the most of any U.S. airline. Pre-overhaul, the cabins had been operating with eight first-class suites and 52 pods in business, though the remodeling is ongoing. American is also flying a subfleet of 787-9 Dreamliners that have 51 business-class suites. (United is putting 64 new suites on that aircraft.)

Part of the allure is that some companies allow employees to book business-class tickets, but not first class.

American Airlines told CNBC it's growing its business-class cabins across the fleet. It's evaluating increasing the number of suites on its smaller long-haul jets, the Boeing 787-8 Dreamliners, from 20 currently to somewhere in the 30s.

"You don't have enough seats to make the seat cost competitive," Nat Pieper, American's chief commercial officer, said in an interview this month. "So what you need to do to make that airplane work is it's got to be a yield play. You need more business-class seats."

Differences between first and business classes on American lately came down to details.

On American's first-class long-haul section, passengers got olives with warm nuts to accompany their beverage early in the flight, while there were no olives for business class.

First class got a soup; business did not. First class got a warm dessert served a la mode in addition to the cold dessert or fruit and cheese plate both cabins were offered.

And there was no memory foam lumbar pillow for business class like there was in first, American said. But otherwise, the experiences were fairly similar for passengers. They still got better food options and service than economy passengers, along with their bigger seats, early boarding and lounge access.

"It's not like the bed can go any flatter than 180 degrees," Harteveldt said. "The bedding can be thicker, and the food nicer."

Delta and United have already replaced international, long-haul first class with dozens of their Delta One and Polaris business lie-flat suites, respectively, each of which is getting upgrades now.

American will continue offering a separate lie-flat first class on its Airbus A321T subfleet it uses for domestic cross-country flying until early 2027. But then that goes away, too.

Meanwhile, airlines over the past decade have added a premium economy section, which sits between business class and economy. It features recliner seats similar to those in domestic first class, often with foot rests, meals served on actual plates, larger seat-back screens than economy and earlier boarding.

Airlines are now cutting up business class into different segments, something they've done for more than a decade with the coach cabin's basic economy, standard and extra legroom seats.

United and Delta this year each announced their most expensive cabins will be sold with basic options, which are more restrictive and don't include things like the ability to pick your seat ahead of time or get access to a special airport lounge.

There are also a few seats in some of the business-class cabins that are roomier, like American's Flagship Suite Preferred and United's Polaris Studio. Even the guest seating in the United suite varies with "standard and companion ottomans," the latter including a seatbelt so you can ask "another Polaris passenger to visit you between takeoff and landing for a drink, chat or game," the airline says.

"I don't think we found the limit on segmentation," United CFO Mike Leskinen told CNBC on Thursday. "We have a few things on the drawing board. We're not done."

Although U.S. carriers are moving away from the designation, first class is far from dead.

Singapore Airlines, Germany's Lufthansa, British Airways and Air France have unveiled updated first-class cabins and other service improvements like food.

British Airways last month said it will build a record 106-suite business class on its Airbus A380 aircraft, the world's largest passenger airplane, but it's also upgrading its first class on board.

Meanwhile, smaller airlines like JetBlue Airways, Frontier Airlines and Allegiant Air are introducing domestic or short-haul first-class seats.

"We're going to be able to do that at a really low price comparatively to what you're used to out there in the marketplace," Frontier Chief Commercial Officer Bobby Schroeter said last month.

#150. Even the price of toys is on the rise. How Americans are scaling back holiday plans

As Amanda Brecht planned how her family would spend Thanksgiving this year, she had to make some difficult decisions.

Because of high gas prices, the 30-year-old decided she wouldn't drive across her home state of Michigan to celebrate with extended family. Instead, Brecht and her husband will spend the holiday with just their three kids. And with food prices rising, they'll be skipping appetizers and serving turkey breast instead of a whole bird.

"Thanksgiving is definitely going to look different this year," Brecht said. "Money doesn't grow on trees. You can only do so much."

With inflation accelerating, Americans like Brecht will see higher prices almost everywhere they look this holiday season. At a time of year synonymous with food, gifts and big spending, many consumers are instead pinching pennies, paring back their plans and making tough choices about how they'll celebrate.

Some are raiding their savings accounts and running up debt on credit cards. They're reporting downbeat outlooks about the economy in surveys. And they've become more selective with spending as inflationary pressures mount.

"Families are facing some tough choices this year," said Alexander Smith, an associate professor who researches behavioral economics at the Worcester Polytechnic Institute. "They might have to cut back on holiday spending in whatever way that they can."

A classic Thanksgiving feast will be harder to pull off as grocery prices rise.

The price of a 10-person Thanksgiving dinner increased 6% to $58.63 in 2026, according to an annual survey from the American Farm Bureau Federation. Faith Parum, the organization's deputy chief economist, said the price increase was a result of higher costs for labor and transportation.

Grocers including Walmart and Aldi have gained traction on social media by offering Thanksgiving meal bundles as a value-focused workaround to higher prices. Walmart's offering promises to feed 10 for $35, and includes a turkey and ingredients to make mashed potatoes, green bean casserole, cranberries, marshmallow-topped sweet potatoes and a pumpkin pie.

Still, some consumers will feel disappointed by having less choice in their menus due to higher prices, according to Joe Bruseulas, chief economist at tax firm RSM.

"We're missing the point here if all we're doing is focusing on the fact that Walmart will sell you a homogeneous dinner meal" at a discount, Bruseulas said. "People feel horrible that they have to go to that because the price of everything has just risen so significantly over the past five years."

Inflation is affecting more than Thanksgiving staples like turkey or stuffing. Food prices on average have jumped around 24% over the last five years, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Some items have seen even sharper increases: Coffee and ground beef, for example, have each climbed by more than 40% in the same period despite pulling back from recent highs.

At the same time, millions of Americans lost SNAP benefits under policy changes included in President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill." Food banks across the U.S. have seen foot traffic skyrocket this year as the reduction in federal nutrition aid coincides with rising prices in grocery aisles.

The Salvation Army said some of its food pantries in October provided double or triple the amount of groceries than they did in the same month a year ago. Demand is now so high that some food banks in Feeding America's national network are worried about running out of supplies.

"We want to make sure that our food banks are as full as they possibly can be going into the holiday season," said Linda Nageotte, Feeding America's president and operations chief. "Far too many of them have empty shelves."

High energy prices as a result of the war with Iran are also affecting Americans' holiday plans.

Slightly fewer Americans will travel at least 50 miles around the Thanksgiving holiday in 2026, marking the first year-over-year decline since the country was in lockdown from the pandemic in 2020, according to AAA. Stacey Barber, a vice president at the auto association, attributed this year's pullback in part to higher fuel costs.

AAA said that about nine out of every 10 Thanksgiving travelers will do so by car. While gas prices have come down from a May peak, they are still up about 18% year over year and could set a new record for the month of November, according to the organization's data.

Travelers are paying about 31% and 23% more for roundtrip domestic airfare around Thanksgiving and Christmas, respectively, than they did a year ago, according to booking platform Hopper. Airlines have blamed the ticket price hikes on rising jet fuel costs in the wake of the war.

"There will be a significant shell shock when they see the price of traveling," said Hayley Berg, lead economist at Hopper.

Berg said price increases would feel more noticeable on routes previously serviced by Spirit, the budget airline that shut down in May. Travelers are booking holiday flights earlier than they did in past years, a sign that prices are top of mind, she said.

Staying home for the holidays isn't a silver-bullet solution, either, as heating costs jump.

The average American household is expected to spend 7% more on home heating this winter than they did last year, according to the National Energy Assistance Directors Association. For the typical family, that equates to an increase of about $66 and a total bill for the season above $1,000.

To be sure, some families will feel it more than others. Heating oil, which is used mainly in homes in the Northeast, has jumped around 70% year over year. However, natural gas, which is one of the most common heating types in the U.S., costs about the same as last year.

Some U.S. states, like Massachusetts and New York, beefed up their home energy support programs to account for higher prices. But Mark Wolfe, NEADA's executive director, said federal aid offerings haven't been meaningfully expanded this year.

"Some of these increases are caused directly by the decision to go to war," Wolfe said. "Why isn't the federal government helping families pay these higher bills?"

The spirit of giving will take on a different meaning for consumers this year.

Retail sales during the all-important holiday season are expected to grow almost 5% this year and surpass $1 trillion for the first time, according to Bain & Company. But the consulting firm said most of that growth would come from inflation, rather than from consumers buying more products.

And Citi on Wednesday said that increased holiday spending would be mainly driven by higher earners this year. The bank said that's a result of the "K"-shaped economy, a term used to describe a scenario where better-off Americans splash out while others struggle.

Toy prices have climbed more than 9% over the past year and now sit at their highest levels since 2019, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Manufacturers have raised prices in response to higher costs for transportation and plastic, according to Chris Byrne, an independent toy industry analyst. The artificial intelligence boom has driven up prices for the chips that power gaming devices and other electronics.

Byrne said that's causing shoppers to focus more on the quality of gifts rather than quantity.

"The toy industry always reflects the culture at large," Byrne said. "It's almost true economically as well. As inflation is rising, people are being a little bit more cautious about what they're buying."

Just 16% of respondents to a Morning Consult survey said they planned to spend more in the 2026 holiday season than they did a year ago. Respondents who were older or had lower earnings were more likely to say their budgets were shrinking, the survey found.

Some are doing away with gifts altogether. A poll from consulting firm TSG and the Electronic Transactions Association found 5% of Americans did not plan to buy any presents in 2026, up three percentage points from last year.

One shopper, Wendy Cooper, set a limit of one gift for each of her three adult children this year. For her teenage daughter, the 56-year-old Atlanta resident has scoured Five Below, the trendy value retailer, for budget-friendly presents.

"It's super stressful," Cooper said. "You have to weigh: Do you guys want lights on and a place to live? Or do you want gifts?"

Across the board, the Bureau of Labor Statistics' consumer price index reaccelerated after the U.S. first struck Iran in late February. Annual inflation rates have begun rising faster than wages in the same period, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Even if Americans keep spending for the holidays, economists caution that high prices during this busy shopping period can further drag on their economic outlooks. In the CNBC Voter Poll early this month, about three-fourths of participants rated the economy as poor or not good. Consumer confidence gauges had dropped significantly this year, but have regained ground in the runup to the holiday shopping season.

From her home in Northern Michigan, Brecht is doing everything she can to make Christmas feel special without breaking the bank.

The graduate student has been delivering DoorDash orders in her spare time and listing clothes on resale platforms to save up money for her kids' holiday gifts. Brecht is watching closely for discounts and will look for potential presents at thrift stores in the coming weeks.

If necessary, Brecht said she would turn to credit cards for some holiday purchases. She isn't alone: TSG and ETA's survey found that about one-third of Americans said they expected to go into debt during the holiday season, a roughly 74% increase from 2025.

"I don't really know how we're going to do it," Brecht said. "I'm just praying for good Black Friday deals."

#147. Trump's rescue of Nvidia's Huang brings political risks

Trump boosts Nvidia boss return but are US politicians on board?

After the AI chipmaker Nvidia announced it was bringing back embattled former head Jensen Huang and the company's stock shot up like a bullet, President Donald Trump took a very public victory lap.

"I made the deal," he said during a lengthy phone interview with cable business news channel CNBC.

Trump followed that with an AI-generated image posted to his social media platform that riffed on the famous photograph taken moments after the 2024 attempt on his life in Pennsylvania. But instead of Trump in the image, Huang was the one surrounded by Secret Service guards. It depicted him in front of an Nvidia banner - with a raised fist clenching a red bra.

The post was classic Trump â potentially offensive, yet still a bit opaque.

Was Trump suggesting Huang survived a scandal â one that includes allegations of recklessly jeopardising US national security by consorting with a suspected Chinese spy â the way he had survived an assassin's bullet?

Was the bra a celebration Huang's reported extramarital affairs? By replacing the US flag with an Nvidia standard, was he claiming that America and one of its largest, most powerful companies are one and the same?

At the very least, the image underscores how inextricably linked Huang and Trump now are, with the fate of the former hinging on the successes and failures of the latter.

And with that comes significant risks for the American president and for Republicans, including Vice-President JD Vance, who played a key part in his efforts to rescue Huang.

While Trump has said that a "very thorough investigation" by federal officials "exonerated" Huang and his reinstatement poses no national security threat, that is far from the final word on the matter.

"Every time you have a Chinese woman that's good looking or beautiful, they say she's a spy," Trump told CNBC, adding that "with Jensen, I don't believe that's true".

Democrats and some concerned Republicans in Congress have a different view.

They are promising to launch their own oversight of Huang and Nvidia, with subpoenas and compelled witness testimony, in order to assess whether they violated laws by allowing China to access sophisticated microchip technology.

State governments could also launch their own investigations.

Any new revelations could further damage the company's standing â and, by association, Trump himself.

"I have warned the administration from the start about Communist China obtaining our most advanced chips through both smuggling and remote access," Republican Senator Tom Cotton posted on X.

"Congress has much to investigate," he said.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

What Jensen Huang's return teaches China's Xi about Trump

US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

While Huang has a long record of success at Nvidia, there is no guarantee that he can recapture past glory now that his star his dimmed.

Even if he is free from any criminal liability, the tech mogul is about to be in a much harsher spotlight than he has ever experienced.

Trump also appears to be on the wrong side of public opinion when it comes to AI writ large.

In the recent US midterm elections, candidates on both the left and right campaigned on imposing new restrictions on data centre construction, with many calling for new legal guardrails to prevent AI-fuelled economic and social disruption.

"Rather than addressing the American people's growing concerns about AI, this administration remains asleep at the wheel or willfully ignorant as our most advanced technologies fall into the hands of our greatest competitors," congresswoman Sydney Kamlager-Dove, a California Democrat on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told the BBC.

The president's own political base is also suspicious of the new technology, with rural conservatives often rebelling at the demands that big tech companies have placed on their local resources and civic infrastructure.

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

The Nvidia story has also exposed rifts in Trump's own administration, with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, a China hawk, and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, a vocal proponent of AI, publicly feuding over whether to impose new restrictions on AI chip exports.

Both Bessent and Lutnick travelled with Trump on his recent trip to China, although it was the commerce secretary who hitched a ride back to the White House on the Marine One helicopter when Trump returned to Washington last Thursday.

Polling also indicated that political corruption was a top issue for voters this year.

The president's willingness to intervene in the deliberations of a major private company in which he owns millions of dollars of stock could further fuel those concerns.

Trump's decision to help save Huang â noting on Monday that "we have to protect our geniuses, and he certainly is a genius" â could further inflame the public's sense that the rich and powerful, genius or not, play by a different set of rules.

"It almost feels like they've bent the rule, the regulation, to allow this to happen because it's such an important part of our economy right now," Steve Bannon, a former top Trump adviser, said on his podcast on Monday.

"Even in the crash of 2008... I never saw the government step in and take over a board of directors."

For this American president, however, these risks appear to be worth taking because of the stakes involved.

Trump has long viewed the success of the US economy as a key part of his political legacy.

He boasted of the historic growth and low unemployment during his first presidential term (while glossing over the disruptions caused by the Covid pandemic).

And since returning to office, he has celebrated as major US stock indices posted new record highs.

Recent US economic expansion has been fuelled largely by investment in AI technology, and Trump has become one of the industry's biggest boosters.

He scolded states and local governments for blocking data centre construction and predicted that the nation that wins the race for supremacy in AI â which he has tried to rebrand as "super intelligence" â will control the fate of the world.

If Trump views the economy as the key to his political success and AI as instrumental to the health of the US economy, then his dramatic, precedent-shattering actions to try to rescue Nvidia, the company at the centre of America's AI industry, make more sense.

In the weeks since concerns over Huang's affair with a suspected Chinese spy first surfaced, Nvidia's stock price tumbled more than 32%.

US stock indices tumbled as well, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq down 10% last week and the larger S&P 500 dropping 8.6%.

The recent financial turmoil may have given Trump a glimpse into a future where AI growth is just the latest financial bubble â like the housing market in 2008 and internet companies in 2001 â that is due for a resounding pop, with similarly dire economic consequences to follow.

Trump's intervention on behalf of Huang arrested that drop, at least for a day.

On Tuesday, Nvidia's stock price gave back much of the bounce it had enjoyed, and larger stock indices were mixed. The fate of the America economy and the AI juggernaut that has powered it is still undetermined.

Trump's intervention may not be a crisis fully averted, but only a crisis delayed.

And if an AI reckoning does come, the US president will be hard-pressed to avoid being caught in the maelstrom.

US to blacklist Asian AI firm Megaspeed after Nvidia scandal

#143. Zoe Kleinman: Nobody is irreplaceable - unless you're Jensen Huang

Remember the tech boss and his head of HR, both married to other people, who were caught on camera at a Coldplay concert with their arms wrapped around each other?

Their horrified reaction went viral, and the pair both resigned. Astronomer, the firm where they both worked, said their actions had fallen short of its standards.

Compared with the scandal which has engulfed Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang in recent weeks, that Coldplay kiss cam moment now feels rather tame.

Huang admitted to having had an affair, reportedly with a woman accused of being a Chinese spy.

There were suggestions that she may have been involved in smuggling Nvidia chips into China, in breach of a US export ban.

Concerns about national security had reached the desk of US President Donald Trump.

There were other stories about some of Huang's relationships with female colleagues.

For most of us, that would without question amount to instant, and irreversible, dismissal.

But while Huang briefly left the company, he was just as swiftly reinstated, and welcomed back with open arms.

As I watched footage of him being greeted with cheers, applause, high fives and a big hug back at the company's HQ on Monday, I wondered how many disgraced bosses would kill to be in his shoes.

"Imagine being that Coldplay CEO reading all the comments on here about how it's OK if you're actually a good enough CEO," quipped menswear writer Derek Guy on X, external.

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

Huang has blown out of the water the old adage that nobody is irreplaceable.

Despite names of potential permanent replacements being bandied around, Nvidia's board evidently reached the same conclusion - interim chief executive Colette Kress was in for less than two weeks.

Well firstly, Huang is the boss of a company that has manoeuvred itself into a uniquely powerful position.

Nvidia's computer chips are considered the best in the business for driving AI.

Demand for its latest tech is through the roof and the firm sets its prices accordingly: Its Rubin and Vera GPUs sell for tens of thousands of dollars each, and one data centre might contain hundreds of them.

So, the US AI sector relies on Nvidia, Trump is determined that the American AI industry needs to be the best in the world, and Huang is the only leader the company has ever known.

He's been at the helm for 33 years and he's a computer scientist himself, renowned for being very hands-on with the development process. (Whether the boss's input is always wanted is another matter, but it's always there.)

Long hours are normal and negative feedback is often given in front of everyone else.

When he wants something to happen, he doesn't stop until it does: one of his early ideas was to halve the chip production cycle, in order to get one step ahead of the competition with their longer lead times for orders.

Lots of experts told him it couldn't be done. Until eventually, they did it.

Not only does Huang have technical expertise, he's also a charismatic figurehead, representing Nvidia at events around the world in his trademark leather jacket, rubbing shoulders with world leaders, royalty, and fellow tech bosses.

For many, he's become something of a cult figure. Nvidia sometimes sells limited edition clothing at conference pop up stalls. A jumper featuring a cartoon image of Huang costs $178 (Â£137).

Nvidia's clothing lines quickly sell out, bought by fans of the tech giant

In addition, Huang has powerful allies, and they are mostly powerful men. US President Donald Trump personally pushed for his return to Nvidia, while Elon Musk said it would be "an honour" to work with him at SpaceX.

He is one of very few people who knows both Trump and China's President Xi Jinping personally - and somehow he has surfed the geopolitical tension between the two continents.

He has said he thinks both China and the US can win the AI race and called on them to pull together.

And finally there's the bottom line, which is always money.

Nvidia value climbs by almost $1tn and tech stocks rally after Jensen Huang returns

Huang's brief departure from Nvidia caused economic turmoil. The firm's value sank by a whopping $1tn before it climbed back up again.

It affected not only pension funds and investors around the world, but also Nvidia's own staff.

Tech employees often get to own valuable stocks in the company they work for as part of their pay packages.

Nvidia employees have been sitting on a goldmine. It wasn't really in their interests to see that drain away because of the boss's love life.

With a firm grip on the company he co-founded, cult-like status in tech circles and friends in high places, Jensen Huang created a situation where even national security concerns over the US's biggest rival couldn't topple him.

US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

#141. Employees at Eric Trump-backed robot startup lose health benefits as company's cash crunch deepens

Employees at Foundation Future Industries were told that their health benefits through administrator Rippling have been terminated, according to a memo obtained by CNBC, adding to mounting signs of financial distress at the humanoid robot startup advised and backed by Eric Trump.

Foundation told staff in a Friday evening email that they'd be offered coverage through COBRA, the federal law that generally lets workers temporarily keep their health insurance after losing benefits by paying the full premium out of pocket.

"Because our last pay run through Rippling was in October, they have ended our benefits with them retroactive to October," a Foundation human resources manager said in the memo. "COBRA will be offered to all employees to cover November."

Foundation has garnered headlines for the bold claims that co-founder Sankaet Pathak — whose previous startup collapsed in 2024 — has made about his robots and their potential use in war zones. Employees were last paid on Oct. 15, and some inside the company are angry at Pathak for a $4.6 million loan he took from his company last year, CNBC reported earlier this month.

A Foundation spokesman didn't immediately respond to an email or phone call seeking comment.

In the staff memo, Foundation blamed Rippling for the disruption to health coverage. The startup also said it would reimburse COBRA premiums paid by workers and that it was onboarding a new benefits provider.

But employees, especially those whose family members are undergoing treatment for medical ailments, worry the company won't have funds to reimburse workers or reinstate coverage, people with direct knowledge of the situation said.

In a statement to CNBC, Rippling indicated it was Foundation's failure to pay for its employee benefits that led to the change: "If a client enrolled in Rippling's [Professional Employer Organization] is unable to meet their payroll and benefits obligations for multiple months, they will first receive a warning and if not met, they are terminated."

Foundation has been seeking a bridge loan to repay employees after its financial woes began in August. It has been in talks with three or four venture capital firms, including existing investor Tribe Capital, Pathak has told employees, according to people familiar with the situation, who asked to remain unnamed because they were not authorized to speak publicly and feared retaliation. Tribe didn't immediately return a request for comment.

The bridge loan would help the company survive until a possible $150 million investment from South Korean conglomerate Base Group lands as early as December or January, Pathak has told employees, the people said.

Base confirmed to Reuters in recent days that it was considering an investment, but the funding has not been finalized.

The potential investment would deepen business ties between the Trump family and the South Korean conglomerate, an aluminum producer also known for real estate and hospitality projects.

Base paid the Trump Organization $2 million in 2025, according to a New York Times article, drawing scrutiny from Democratic senators over conflict-of-interest concerns. Base has also pursued other business relationships with the Trump Organization, including a Trump-branded golf course in South Korea.

Eric Trump, who has invested in Foundation and was given the title of chief strategy advisor, didn't immediately respond to a CNBC email seeking comment.

The prospect of employees having to pay for their own health coverage comes as many are already drawing down savings to cover living expenses. With another scheduled payday approaching on Nov. 30, workers are facing a third straight pay cycle without income.

Many are actively interviewing for other jobs, and attendance at the company's San Francisco headquarters has fallen sharply, according to people familiar.

Frustration has boiled over into distrust of Pathak's leadership for some employees, while others hope Pathak can secure funding to repay workers and bring the company back to financial health. In conversations and memos, Pathak says that he is highly invested in securing Foundation's future, the people said.

Despite the firm's shaky finances, Foundation continued to hire for open roles until recently and some new workers have never been fully paid, said the people.

To keep employees from leaving before a possible Base Group investment, Pathak has floated the idea of offering workers equity in the startup, the people said.

At the same time, human resources staff have warned workers against taking legal action with California to recover their wages, saying that any employees who file complaints with the state would be dismissed, the people said.

Following a Nov. 14 CNBC report detailing the missed payrolls and a $4.6 million loan Pathak took from the startup in 2025, the chief executive addressed the issue in an all-hands meeting, according to the people.

Pathak defended the arrangement by telling workers that he had plowed his own money into the startup and takes only a nominal salary, the people said.

The co-founder also told employees who were in dire financial straits to contact human resources for help covering personal bills, the people added.

"I'm burning through savings just to afford my apartment," one employee told CNBC. "It's extremely unfortunate we're in this situation."

#140. What will the energy cap changes mean for my bills?

Energy bills are to soar in January, as prices governed by regulator Ofgem's cap rise at the coldest time of the year.

The cap affects millions of people in England, Scotland and Wales, and reflects higher wholesale gas costs which have been pushed up by the US-Israel war with Iran.

It means people on variable tariffs should give an accurate meter reading, ensure they are receiving all the support to which they are entitled, and take care over energy use.

The energy cap fixes the maximum amount which customers on standard variable tariffs can be charged for each unit of gas and electricity used.

It covers more than 20 million households in England, Wales and Scotland and is set by the regulator Ofgem every three months, external.

It affects those paying by direct debit, by standard credit (they pay when they get a bill), or prepayment meters in slightly different ways.

Most of the increase in the energy price cap has, and will be, driven by higher gas prices.

The government's decision to temporarily remove VAT from domestic electricity bills has cut about Â£45 a year from a typical bill.

Meanwhile, millions of households will receive a Â£100 automatic payment to help cover winter costs.

Customers' actual bills depend on the amount of energy they use and how they pay for it.

From January, for those on variable tariffs:

For electricity, average unit prices will go up on 1 January from 26.32p per kilowatt-hour to 32.69p

Gas will increase from 7.97p per kilowatt-hour to 10.96p, higher than in the wake of the invasion of Ukraine

Daily standing charges will be 54.77p for electricity and 29.65p for gas

The roughly 11 million households on fixed tariffs will not see their prices change until the end of their fixed term.

Ofgem regulates the energy market in England, Scotland and Wales. Northern Ireland has a separate system.

As well as how much energy you use and how you pay for it, where you live, the type of property you have, how energy efficient it is, how many people live there, and the weather all make a difference.

The Ofgem cap is illustrated using "typical" household energy use in a year with a single bill for gas and electricity settled by direct debit.

The vast majority of people pay their bill this way to help spread payments across the year.

In July, Ofgem reduced what it believes to be a "typical" level of energy use, because many homes have cut back due to high prices in recent years while energy efficiency has improved.

Its new estimate is 9,500 kWh of gas and 2,500 kWh of electricity a year.

Submitting a meter reading when the cap changes means you are not charged for estimated usage at the wrong rate.

This is especially important when prices go up.

Customers with working smart meters do not need to submit a reading as their bill is calculated automatically.

Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Ofgem also controls standing charges, which are a fixed daily fee to cover the costs of connecting households to gas and electricity supplies. These vary slightly by region, external and payment method.

Between 1 October and 31 December 2026, average standing charges for direct debit customers are 54.83p a day for electricity and 29.68p a day for gas.

From January, daily standing charges will be 54.77p for electricity and 29.65p for gas.

The electricity standing charge is lower than the amount charged over the previous three months, while the gas charge is largely unchanged.

Campaigners have long argued that standing charges are unfair because they make up a bigger proportion of the bill for low-energy households. Others say it just shifts the cost from one part of the bill to another rather than cutting it.

The government has announced plans to remove gas standing charges, although no details have been published on how that will happen

Fixed deals provide certainty for a set period â often a year, or longer â but if energy prices drop while you are on the deal, you could be stuck at a higher price.

You may also have to pay a penalty to leave a fixed deal early if you change your mind.

Experts recommend checking whole-of-market energy price comparison sites to help find the best deal and studying the details of exit fees.

Since 1 April, energy bill charges related to the low-income household insulation scheme - called the Energy Company Obligation - have been scrapped, and for three years, renewable energy projects will be 75%-funded by general taxation instead of a levy on energy bills.

Nearly everyone in England, Wales and Scotland will benefit from this cut, although the amounts will vary between households.

However, the cost of maintaining and strengthening energy network infrastructure like power lines, cables and gas pipes is rising.

In December 2025, Ofgem approved a Â£28bn investment to improve the electricity and gas grids in Great Britain to strengthen energy supplies and protect customers against volatile energy prices. It will also reduce Britain's dependence on gas.

Customers will pay part of the cost of the upgrade, through an additional Â£108 added to energy bills by 2031.

These charges started to appear from April 2026, adding about Â£6 a month to the bill for a typical household covered by the energy cap.

High electricity bills targeted in planned shakeup to energy pricing

Households could get free electricity for doing washing on sunny weekends

Suppliers must offer customers affordable payment plans or repayment holidays if they are struggling to pay their bills. Most also offer hardship grants.

Since the Russian invasion of Ukraine in 2022, bills have gone up by about 70% compared with the pre-crisis norm, and since then unpaid bills and charges have shot up.

Vanessa Northam, director at debt charity StepChange, has advice for people who may be struggling:

Take stock of all household money coming in and going out.

Look closely at how much energy you are using, and be alert to estimated bills, which might prove to be inaccurate.

Tell your supplier if you think you are going to struggle.

It is often best to try to talk to someone who can fully understand your situation.

Energy UK has a list of support schemes., external

Heating oil is not covered by the energy cap. About 1.5 million UK households use heating oil and they have already seen a sharp increase in bills since the Middle East conflict triggered a jump in oil prices. Some users have seen costs more than double.

The issue is particularly acute in Northern Ireland, where about 500,000 homes use heating all, almost two-thirds of all households.

In March, the government announced a Â£53m support package to help low-income households in rural communities who use heating oil.

Starmer announces Â£53m support to help with heating oil costs

How are rising costs caused by the Iran war affecting your quality of life or business?

#137. 'I only use the washing machine on a Sunday' - how we're keeping our energy bills down

Domestic energy prices will soar by 26% in January under the regulator Ofgem's new price cap, pushing up bills for millions of households across England, Scotland and Wales.

With household budgets already under pressure, finding ways to reduce gas and electricity costs has become increasingly important. But how much difference can simple changes really make?

The BBC spoke to three people about what they're doing to keep their energy costs down.

Kelly Anne Harvey uses electric blankets and portable oil radiators in some rooms to save on the central heating

Like many people, Kelly Anne Harvey, a building surveyor from Cleveleys near Blackpool, is worried about her energy bill going up next year.

The 44-year-old, who is married with a five-year-old son, has a combined household income of Â£60,000.

She currently pays about Â£154 a month for gas and electricity on a standard variable rate with Octopus, but is looking for a fixed-rate deal to offset the rise.

The family fell into arrears on their energy bills a few years ago and Kelly says she won't let it happen again.

To control her costs she pays by direct debit, which spreads her payments and is typically Â£138 cheaper a year than paying on receipt of a bill.

The family uses electric blankets and portable oil radiators in some rooms so they don't have to turn the central heating on as often.

They also take part in Octopus's energy saving scheme, which rewards customers who use less electricity at peak times, such as early evenings, with a free hour of energy at the weekend.

"On Sunday we'll turn on the dishwasher, tumble drier, washing machine and maybe even charge our electric cars," says Kelly.

Stacey says she keeps the curtains closed "to keep the heat in" and her daughters wear thermal clothing

Stacey, 40, a single mother from Bristol, says dealing with rising energy costs has felt like a "battle" recently and she's worried about 2027.

The office worker, whose daughters are aged 10 and 12, is on a fixed-term tracker tariff with E.On Next, which charges rates set below the quarterly Ofgem price cap. She expects to pay Â£1,800 for energy this year, up Â£400 on last year.

Stacey, who is part of the Changing Realities project at Glasgow University, which documents life for people on low incomes, has tried a number of things to control her energy costs.

She's turned her central heating down from 22C to 19.5C, bought electric blankets and pays her bills by direct debit.

She also leaves her curtains shut to "keep the heat in", while her daughters wear thermal clothing if it gets chilly in the house. The family is also using less hot water.

"I've got girls and they're growing up and they want to spend loads of time in the shower [but] it's just not possible."

Energy bills to rise by 26% from January after gas prices surge

Three reasons why your energy bill is so high

Will I get the Â£100 energy payment and when will I receive it?

Around 1.5 million households in the UK rely on heating oil for heating and hot water, and their costs have already soared in the past year due to disruption in global oil markets.

"When I first moved here two years ago you could get a litre of heating oil for 50-60p," says Bradley Knowles, 32, who lives in a barn conversion with his husband, mother and sister in rural Lincolnshire.

"At the height of the Iran war it went up to Â£1.20 or Â£1.30 and now it's stabilised at around Â£1."

To cut costs, the finance worker orders oil in bulk, buying 1,000-1,500 litres, preferably in the summer months when prices are lower. He also tries to order a few weeks in advance to avoid expensive next-day deliveries.

Bradley uses comparison sites to see which local suppliers offer the cheapest oil and puts money aside each month so he can always afford his orders.

Oil-powered boilers may take time to heat up, but Bradley advises against leaving them on all day as that can be very expensive.

Instead, he uses an electric tank to store his warm water and tops up the heating in parts of the property with electric radiators.

#136. ‘Your booth is waiting’: Denny’s welcomes Jensen Huang back as Nvidia CEO

Jensen Huang is back at the helm of Nvidia — and Denny's has a booth waiting for him.

The American diner chain welcomed the billionaire's return as Nvidia CEO by revealing that it had reinstated a commemorative plaque marking the birthplace of the artificial intelligence chip giant after learning of its removal on Nov. 13.

"Some things you just don't take off the menu. Jensen Huang is one of them," Denny's said in a post on X.

The plaque marks the booth at a Denny's restaurant in Silicon Valley, California, where Huang and his co-founders discussed the idea for Nvidia, which was founded in 1993.

The restaurant chain said it learned on Nov. 13, a day after Huang's resignation, that the plaque had been removed.

"We put the plaque right back up where it belongs," Denny's said, without specifying who had removed it or why.

Huang's ties to the diner chain, however, stretch back well before Nvidia's founding. He worked at Denny's as a teenager, washing dishes and serving customers before going on to build one of the world's most valuable companies.

The restaurant chain also disclosed that it had contacted Nvidia's communications team on Nov. 10 to express its pride in their shared history.

Huang's return came less than two weeks after his sudden resignation rattled investors and raised questions about corporate governance and succession planning at the chipmaker.

While his return has put those issues in the spotlight, Denny's offered a simpler message to its former employee.

"Jensen, your booth is waiting and the coffee's on. We're always open, 24/7," the restaurant chain said.

#134. Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Keeping yourself warm by wearing thick clothes, using blankets and having hot drinks can help reduce heating bills

Energy bills will soar by 26% in the new year as the war in Iran pushes up wholesale prices.

Typical household bills will rise by Â£37 per month as Ofgem's latest price cap comes into force from January to March, but experts say it is possible to keep rising bills in check.

Here are four areas to consider ahead of the increase.

Ofgem's price cap, as well as suppliers' fixed deals, set a limit on the price of each unit of energy, not the total bill.

So, you pay for what you use. Millions of people have cut back on their energy consumption during years of relatively high prices.

With the unit price of gas, in particular, set to soar, experts say you should take a fresh look at your energy use and renew good habits.

turning down the flow temperature on a combi boiler - usually indicated by a radiator icon, to make it operate more efficiently

turning off the pre-heat facility on some combi boilers, which would have kept some water heated up when not in use

turning down the thermostat one degree, although 18C is the recommended minimum

lowering radiator valves outside of the living room

These are small changes but - alongside washing clothes at 30C, blocking draughts and batch cooking - they can reduce energy use and bills.

Ofgem says about five million people still pay their bill when it arrives every three months, rather than receive a monthly bill often initially based on an estimate.

Taking regular meter readings, or having a smart meter, while paying by monthly direct debit solves that problem.

Paying by direct debit costs significantly less than by so-called standard credit - typically Â£138 cheaper a year.

Customers on prepayment schemes - usually people on low incomes struggling to pay bills - used to pay a higher rate than others, but now they pay slightly less than the capped direct debit variable rate.

Various deals may rely on paying by direct debit or having a smart meter.

Moving to a fixed energy tariff means the price you are charged per unit of gas or electricity does not change every three months, although the total bill will still vary depending on how much energy you use.

The trouble is that it is very difficult to work out whether a fixed deal is a good deal at the moment, for those renewing fixed deals or looking for one for the first time.

Some fixed deals are available at unit prices below the next forecast price cap level. But should energy prices fall sharply then a longer-term fix might not seem such good value compared with the price cap.

So consumer group Which? also recommends paying close attention to any exit fees when picking a fixed deal. If variable prices fall below your fixed rate and you want to get out of it before the end of your agreement period, you may have to pay a hefty sum.

Before you fix, consumer website MoneySavingExpert advises you look at all the deals available, not just what your own supplier is offering.

The government will give a Â£100 payment to millions of homes to help cover winter energy costs. It will go to homes in council tax bands A to D in England, with the equivalent expected in Scotland and Wales.

There are also a range of other forms of support specifically for people on low incomes.

This winter anyone on means-tested benefits will get Â£150 off their electricity bill.

Your supplier will apply the discount automatically unless you are in Scotland, where you will need to contact your energy company to ask for the help.

Some people will also be eligible for a Cold Weather Payment, external and most pensioners will receive the winter fuel payment.

Beyond that, most energy suppliers offer hardship grants for people struggling to pay their bills and the government's Fuel Direct Scheme, external can help you repay energy debt.

You can get more advice from Ofgem, external, Citizens Advice, external, or your own energy supplier. Trade body Energy UK has a list of support schemes, external.

People on low incomes may also be eligible for grants to help with insulating their homes, and there are local schemes offering grants for solar panels, heat pumps and the like.

But if you are hunting around for grants, watch out for scams pretending to be official money-off schemes.

How is the cost of energy affecting your quality of life? What questions do you have?

#131. ESPN, NFL to show Super Bowl 61 in Imax theaters

Super Bowl 61 will be telecast live in hundreds of Imax theaters in February via a partnership with the NFL and Disney's ESPN, which has the broadcast rights to the 2027 game.

It marks the largest Imax live event in the company's history and the first time that the Super Bowl will be available in the format, according to a news release.

The four-hour event on Feb. 14 will include ESPN's main Super Bowl game telecast, as well as Jay-Z's halftime performance and pregame and postgame coverage.

Tickets will be available for theaters in the United States and select international markets, including Australia, New Zealand and Brazil. Tickets are expected to go on sale in January. Pricing was not disclosed.

"Watching the Super Bowl with your fellow fans is one of the great shared experiences in sports, and this first-of-its-kind collaboration with the NFL and IMAX takes it to a historic new scale," said Ken Potrock, president of major event integration for The Walt Disney Co.

In addition to ESPN channels, this season's championship game will also be available on streaming platform Disney+ as well as Disney's broadcast network ABC.

#130. Trump's accounts traded Nvidia shares 44 times this year, records show

Donald Trump's investment portfolios have bought or sold shares in chipmaker Nvidia at least 44 times this year, a review of the US president's financial disclosures shows.

Trump's most recent public declarations of his investment trading show that between January and September the portfolios purchased between $5.2m and $17m (Â£4m and Â£13m) in Nvidia stock - and sold between $4.4m and $12m. His portfolios held between $12.5m and $58m of Nvidia shares at the end of 2025.

Financial disclosures to the Office of Government Ethics report a low and high range of transaction values, so a more precise analysis of how much Nvidia stock was traded is not possible.

The period of recent turmoil at Nvidia and volatility in its share price is not covered by the disclosures. In the last two weeks, co-founder and chief executive Jensen Huang resigned and then Trump played a significant part in his reinstatement.

The White House has said that Trump's stock and bond portfolio is independently managed by third parties and neither he nor his family "has any ability to direct, influence or provide input regarding how the portfolio is invested or when investments are bought or sold".

Huang resigned on 12 November after reports of extramarital affairs, including with one woman who has alleged ties to Chinese intelligence. Nvidia's share price dived following news of his resignation.

But on Monday Nvidia's board announced the 63-year-old was being reinstated as CEO.

Trump told US media he had "made the deal" to "convince" the tech giant's board to bring Huang back. The board said it had "received input from senior levels of the United States government" that he posed "no national security risk".

Nvidia's share price surged 31% on Monday following Huang's return. About $1tn (Â£763bn) was added to Nvidia's market value, but the company's shares are still trading below their value before reports of Huang's alleged affairs began to circulate.

However, concerns remain among both Republican and Democratic politicians about Huang's actions and Nvidia's compliance with US controls over the export of advanced computer chips to China, as well as Trump's involvement in getting him reappointed as CEO.

Nvidia's chip technology and investments have been critical to the growth of the artificial intelligence (AI) industry, leading to it becoming one of the world's most valuable companies.

The firm now represents more than 6% of the S&P 500 share index, according to State Street financial services. This makes it the second most valuable company on the index after Apple, whose shares Trump's accounts also frequently trade.

Nvidia also ranks highly in many global indices, meaning that investment accounts held around the world, including pensions, are likely to include its shares.

Our analysis of Trump's Nvidia trades does not include other investment products held by him which may include its shares.

Trump made at least $2.2bn (Â£1.7bn) from a variety of investments and commercial activities, according to the financial disclosure report for the first year of Trump's second term.

Barbara Perry, a presidential historian at the University of Virginia's Miller Center, told the BBC there is "no precedent for this," adding: "It's beyond anything we've ever seen in the presidency."

#127. Energy bills to rise by 26% from January after gas prices surge

Watch: Martin Lewis' tips on staying warm this winter

Energy bills will soar by 26% for millions of households in January after a surge in gas prices sparked by the Iran war.

A typical bill will increase by about Â£37 a month in the first three months of the new year, although the price will depend on how much energy a household uses, so it could be closer to Â£60 extra a month in winter.

A household using a typical amount of energy in England, Scotland and Wales and paying by direct debit, would see their annual bill rise from Â£1,723 to Â£2,166 if the increase was sustained for a year, said regulator Ofgem.

The government will pay millions of households Â£100 from 4 January to help cover winter costs on top of an existing VAT cut on electricity.

Charities and suppliers expect debt to rise and a wave of people to need emergency support.

Ofgem said the rise had been driven entirely by higher wholesale costs faced by suppliers owing, primarily, to the Iran war. Wholesale gas prices, paid by suppliers, were up 33% in the last three months, the period used to set the January to March cap. They have more than doubled since late 2025.

The warm home discount will take Â£150 off bills for six million households on benefits this winter. There have been calls for this support, funded through everyone's bill, to be extended.

"We are staring down the barrel of a brutal winter," said Dame Clare Moriarty, chief executive of Citizens Advice.

Neil Kenward, Ofgem's director general for markets, said it wrote to energy suppliers "reminding them of their responsibilities" to customers this winter. They have been told to identify people who need help early, make support easy to access and ensure repayment plans are affordable and sustainable.

Campaign Groups Friends of the Earth and Global Witness said major oil and energy companies are making huge profits because of spiralling oil costs, hurting households.

Energy UK, which represents suppliers, said the firms which send your bill, have profits capped on a percentage basis and do not see the same benefit as international energy generating companies. However, higher bills will mean higher profits in nominal cash terms.

The increase is the biggest since Russia's invasion of Ukraine drove up prices. It comes at a tough time of year, when the weather is at its coldest.

People on prepayment meters will be hit as their spending comes when energy is needed, rather than the cost being spread over the year as with direct debits.

The cap sets a maximum price for each unit of energy, not the total bill. Those on fixed deals are unaffected until the end of their term, but for those on variable tariffs it means:

For electricity, average unit prices will go up on 1 January from 26.32p per kilowatt-hour to 32.69p. The electricity price is often pegged to gas, owing to gas-fired generation.

Gas will increase from 7.97p per kilowatt-hour to 10.96p, higher than in the wake of the invasion of Ukraine.

Daily standing charges will be 54.77p for electricity and 29.65p for gas.

The cap is set every three months, based on the costs faced by suppliers as well as the cost of running the energy system.

It is based on a three-month period that ends about six weeks before the new cap comes into force. A fall in international wholesale prices in recent days will not be reflected in January's cap, but it will be in April's.

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh said people will be "deeply concerned" by another rise in energy bills.

"For too long, British families have been paying the price for conflicts overseas and volatile fossil fuel markets," she said.

Andrew Bowie, shadow energy secretary, said: "It is an outrage that, as bills soar, Labour are not only piling green levies and taxes onto bills but signing us up to an EU scheme that risks pushing them even higher.

Stacey says she keeps the curtains closed "to keep the heat in" and her daughters wear thermal clothing

Stacey, 40, a single mother from Bristol, said dealing with rising energy costs has felt like a "battle" recently and she's worried about 2027.

She's turned her central heating down from 22C to 19.5C, bought electric blankets and pays her bills by direct debit.

She also leaves her curtains shut to "keep the heat in", while her daughters wear thermal clothing if it gets chilly in the house.

'I only use the washing machine on a Sunday' - how we're keeping our energy bills down

Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Will I get the Â£100 energy payment and when will I receive it?

Up to 20 million households in England will get a Â£100 payment from 4 January to help with their energy bills this winter, with equivalent payments in Scotland and Wales.

Citizens Advice said the money will disappear quickly, particularly for those on prepayment meters, leading to a wave of requests for emergency help.

Energy UK said the funding was poorly targeted and insufficient to stem rising levels of customer debt.

The government said councils had a Â£100m discretionary fund to support vulnerable households which fell outside of the main scheme.

#125. Guitar wars - the transatlantic battle over the Stratocaster

The Fender Stratocaster is widely considered to be the best-known shape in electric guitars

Hans Thomann leads the way through his music store in central Germany to the large electric guitar department, where the walls are hung with hundreds of instruments.

Brands on show include famous names Fender and Gibson, plus Harley Benton.

The latter is a low-price range of guitars owned by the family-run company that Thomann leads â Thomann, one of the world's largest online retailers of musical instruments.

The shop, together with warehouses and offices, are at the company's headquarters in the tiny village of Treppendorf in rural Upper Franconia, in Bavaria.

The business was founded by Thomann's father, Hans Thomann Senior, in 1954.

In one corner of the store, a young man is trying out a Fender Stratocaster, one of the world's most iconic electric guitars.

Designed in the US in the early 1950's, the "Strat" became the instrument of choice for many famous rock and pop musicians, such as Jimi Hendrix, Eric Clapton and Buddy Holly.

Hendrix famously set one on fire at the Monterey Pop Festival in 1967, and his performances helped make the guitar a symbol of modern rock music.

Hans Thomann says that Fender's letter came as a big surprise

These days, the Stratocaster is very much on Hans Thomann's mind, because it's the subject of an intense copyright dispute.

When the guitar was first made, California-based Fender did not trademark the body shape. So, since the 1960s Strat-shaped guitars have been made and sold around the world by countless manufacturers.

The shape is so ubiquitous that many musicians consider it to be a generic design.

But since March of this year, Fender has begun a new campaign to defend its right to the guitar's sinuous shape. It follows a decision by a court in DÃ¼sseldorf last December which granted Fender copyright protection, external of the Stratocaster design in Germany.

The defendant, Chinese seller Yiwu Philharmonic Musical Instruments, did not appear. The DÃ¼sseldorf court accepted Fender's arguments and ruled in its favour. According to German law the court makes its decision based on the facts presented by the plaintiff if the defendant doesn't raise objections.

The ruling said that Fender's design for the Stratocaster was "an outstanding, free creative achievement" that "clearly reflects the personality of its author".

It described the guitar as "a body without edges" with "soft curves that evoke associations with the female torso consisting of hips, waist and arms".

On its website, Fender announced in March, external the decision created "enforceable rights" against any guitars using the Strat body shape, that are manufactured, sold or distributed into Germany or other EU countries, "regardless of where those guitars are produced".

Fender began to send out cease and desist letters to dozens of retailers and manufacturers, both large and small, asking them to stop making and selling Strat lookalikes heading for Europe.

One of these letters, seen by the BBC, said Fender "insists that you immediately stop manufacturing, marketing, selling and producing the infringing products".

The letter also demanded that companies recall and destroy the guitars.

Jimi Hendrix helped to make the Fender Stratocaster famous

The move caused an outcry in the guitar world, among manufacturers and musicians.

"Time to boycott Fender," one person wrote on the Facebook page of the boutique US guitar maker, LSL Instruments.

Thomann received a cease-and-desist letter shortly after a visit to Treppendorf in March by Fender's chief executive Bud Cole.

"We remember the meeting as a very positive and constructive exchange," says Thomann. "At the time we had no indication that Fender was considering legal action."

"For more than 70 years, we have carried Fender instruments with great appreciation for the brand and many of us are Fender players ourselves. We were therefore surprised and disappointed by Fender's recent actions."

In 2025, Thomann sold over 112,000 Strat-style guitars, including more than 16,000 made by Fender and Fender subsidiary brand Squier.

"As a retailer, we want to continue offering our customers everything that is available in the world of guitars," adds Thomann. "We would have welcomed to the opportunity to discuss the matter openly before receiving a formal legal demand for such extreme action."

Thomann has just one physical shop, but sells online around the world

In June, Thomann the company started legal action against Fender, filing an "action of non-infringement" that said that Fender does not have a valid copyright claim to the Stat shape.

Thomann says that the DÃ¼sseldorf ruling was simply a default judgement based on missed deadlines and didn't represent a "comprehensive review of the legal claims".

For its part, Fender, which is majority-owned by Hawaii-based company Servco Pacific, says it has a responsibility to protect the Stratocaster's iconic design. In July, it sued Thomann in a German court, for copyright infringement.

In a statement to the BBC, Fender said it remained "confident" in the original DÃ¼sseldorf court judgement.

"Our preference has always been to keep lines of communication open and resolve these matters through constructive dialogue rather than litigation," it added.

With the legal cases continuing, Thomann says he believes it is important to get "legal clarity â not only for us but for the wider guitar industry."

"Ultimately, we want to help preserve an environment in which innovation, diversity and creative development can continue to thrive," he adds.

The Stratocaster is the most used emoji for a guitar

Thomann says he was taking legal action not just for his company, but for smaller music companies too.

"We used to be a small music shop ourselves," he says.

"Many of the smaller manufacturers, guitar makers and dealers affected simply don't have the financial and legal resources to conduct a legal dispute like this."

One such company is LSL Instruments, a family-owned boutique guitar maker in California. Its director of sales, Johny Miller, told me that when they first received Fender's letter they were "pretty terrified and blown away".

"They were asking us to retrieve guitars from dealers out there and destroy them and send them proof.

"So when we heard Hans Thomann got the same letter and responded by suing themâ¦ so that everybody in the guitar community could understand exactly what's going onâ¦ we were so thankful and grateful," he says.

Fender tells BBC that it agrees that "legal clarity was important", and that it was now focusing more on targeting larger manufacturers.

It also says it realises that "our outreach has caused concern and frustration, particularly among smaller, independent guitar builders. "To them we say: we continue to hear you, and we're listening."
