#123. Norse Atlantic flights suspended after warning finances 'critical'

Some Norse Atlantic flights were cancelled on Wednesday and Thursday, raising concerns about the future of the low-cost long-haul airline shortly after it warned that it was in a "critical financial situation."

Shares of Norse closed 53.3% lower on Wednesday after plunging as much as 86% during the session.

The airline had been set to operate two Pakistan International Airlines flights Wednesday under a wet-lease agreement, in which one airline provides the aircraft and crew to another. The agreement had only been signed in early October.

But PK786 from London to Islamabad and PK710 from Manchester to Lahore were cancelled because of "an unexpected operational suspension," the Pakistani flag carrier said on X.

Norse Atlantic has yet to make an official statement on its operational status. Checks by CNBC showed the airline was still selling tickets as at 1:30 a.m. Oslo time on Thursday.

PIA said it would operate the routes using its own Boeing 777 aircraft from Wednesday.

Norse's own flight Z0 783 from London Gatwick to Orlando was also shown as canceled Wednesday on live air traffic website Flightradar24. London Gatwick's website listed the flight's status for Thursday as "enquire airline."

In a bid to stave off financial troubles, Norse cut 75 jobs in May as part of an effort to reduce costs by $50 million, after Eivind Roald replaced founder Bjorn Tore Larsen as CEO in November last year.

The Norwegian carrier said Wednesday that its situation depended on securing financing for short-term liquidity needs and that it was in discussions about a potential strategic takeover.

"Norse is also engaged in discussions with selected parties on short term financing, however, there can be no assurances that such discussions will result in a viable solution," the airline said.

India's largest airline, low-cost carrier IndiGo, entered an agreement with Norse Atlantic in early 2025 to lease six 787-9 aircraft as it launched flights to Europe and Britain ahead of the arrival of its own widebody planes.

The agreement concluded at the end of October this year, with IndiGo citing airspace constraints, elevated fuel costs and currency pressures.

At its peak in 2024, Norse Atlantic had 4,822 scheduled flights. That number dipped to 4,695 in 2025 and 2,565 in 2026, according to aviation analyst Cirium. The airline had 122 flights scheduled for November this year.

Norse Atlantic CEO Roald told CNBC in May that he was not "overly concerned" about access to fuel and said the airline had no indication at the time that the crisis would have an immediate impact. The airline said in October that it was continuing to operate at reduced capacity because of "prolonged high fuel prices."

#122. US stocks fall further as fears over AI intensify

Big technology firms connected to AI continued to see share prices fall on Wednesday, with stock in Nvidia and Anthropic dropping sharply in early trading.

The surge in Nvidia shares after boss Jensen Huang was reinstated was temporary, and the general stock sell-off continued, with all three main US stock market indices down by closing time. The tech-dominated Nasdaq closed 0.6% lower.

The declines came amid concerns over the level and sustainability of AI investment, which has rattled investors in recent weeks.

Shares in other major tech firms, including Amazon, Tesla and Microsoft closed the day lower, though Meta and SpaceX were in positive territory by the end of the trading day.

Nvidia's share price was down 8% in early trading on Wednesday, extending Tuesday's slide as the boost from Huang's return eased. At close, it ended up 3.4% lower.

The resignation and subsequent reinstatement of Nvidia's CEO after revelations of his extramarital affairs has seen the market value of the world's premier AI chipmaker fluctuate wildly.

Given Nvidia's position as both supplier and financier of many other firms involved in the AI rollout, its rocky fortnight has had a wider impact.

The US government also announced a raft of new tariffs on countries and trading partners on Wednesday as part of President Donald Trump's flagship trade policy.

The value of big tech companies have sheltered the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs.

But share prices in recent weeks have been volatile in response to the US midterm elections and other events in the AI industry.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

Investors have taken fright at Democrat gains in the US midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms and even a moratorium on the development of new data centres.

Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will eventually pay off.

"It seems that these companies are trying to one-up each other on the amount of money that they're spending, but you have to show that it's worth it, and that's where Wall Street is saying I don't know," said Todd Schoenberger, chief investment officer at CrossCheck Management.

Despite concerns, investment in AI is expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, which said the technology will remain the "dominant driver of business investment growth" next year.

Shares in Anthropic continued to trade below the $83 they debuted at last week, while Amazon shares fell by just over 3%.

The declines come ahead of the US stock market closing for Thanksgiving on Thursday before opening for a shortened session on Friday.

#120. K-beauty stocks rally as Korean brands top Amazon bestseller lists globally ahead of Black Friday

South Korean beauty stocks jumped Thursday as the country's skincare brands dominated Amazon's bestseller rankings ahead of Black Friday.

Shares of K-beauty giant APR climbed 3.77%, while cosmetic contracts manufacturers Kolmar Korea and Cosmax gained 5.46% and 2.91%, respectively.

APR shares, in particular, have rallied sharply this week, up 28%, defying a broader market sell-off in Seoul markets, with the benchmark Kospi index down 2.65% during the same period.

A cosmetic sector index tracked by Meritz Securities had gained 12% this week until Wednesday, according to a Thursday note from the brokerage.

The rally reflects renewed optimism over earnings growth following strong performances by K-beauty brands across major e-commerce platforms in the U.S. and Europe with Black Friday sales already underway, as well as increasingly attractive valuations following months of share price declines, according to Meritz.

K-beauty products topped Amazon's skincare bestseller rankings across six major markets — the U.S., the U.K., Germany, France, Spain and Italy — as of Nov. 25, according to data compiled by Meritz Securities. Other Korean beauty brands, including Biodance, Illiyoon and Anua, also ranked among the top 10 in the U.S., the data showed.

As of Thursday morning, Korean beauty products occupied several of the top spots in Amazon's U.S. beauty and personal care category, the e-commerce website showed.

Meritz Securities expects APR shares to maintain their positive momentum through the year-end, with a more substantial rebound in early 2027.

The brokerage named APR and Cosmax as its top picks in the cosmetics sector. Korean beauty brands have established an "overwhelming presence" in the bestseller rankings of major e-commerce platforms during Black Friday sales, prompting the market to reassess the growth's potential, the brokerage said.

#118. US approves SpaceX plan to send a million data satellites to space

SpaceX is an aerospace company that builds rockets and spacecraft, as well as running satellite services

US regulators have given SpaceX the go-ahead to launch a million satellites into Earth's orbit to help build data centres in space - the first approval of its kind.

The company, which is owned by Elon Musk, wants to use sunlight to power satellites that will process data for AI and send the results back to Earth.

The Federal Communications Commission (FCC) said on Wednesday that the project, known as Starmind, will "support America's booming super intelligence economy".

Its approval comes as communities across the US have pushed back against the expansion of data centres on the ground.

Space X believes the "orbital data centres" are the most cost and energy-efficient way to meet the growing demand for artificial intelligence (AI) computing power that is beyond "terrestrial capabilities".

FCC Chairman Brendan Carr said in a statement that the project "opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America's leadership and growth".

"This is the kind of American ingenuity that can unite our strengths in space and advanced technology, reinforce our economic leadership, and expand prosperity here at home," Carr said.

Responding to the announcement, Musk wrote on X: "Much appreciated. This will be a great asset to America and the world."

Under the FCC licence, deployment of the satellites has to begin no later than November 2033. SpaceX will be required to have deployed 10% by November 2035, rising to 50% by November 2038.

The company has already said it wants to launch its first Starmind satellite next year.

Its unclear if a million satellites will be launched or if SpaceX has sought approval for a higher number than it will use.

Like SpaceX's current Starlink network, which provides high-speed internet via more than 10,000 satellites, Starmind's satellites will operate in low-Earth orbit at altitudes ranging between 500-2,000km (310-1,242 miles).

Starmind's satellites will communicate with Starlink's and then relay the data to Earth.

The solar-powered satellite system "promises to bring the human race closer to a Kardashev II civilisationâone that can harness the full power of the sun", the FCC statement said, in a reference to a hypothetical society, proposed by an astronomer in the 1960s, capable of harnessing the entire energy output of its home star.

The orbiting data centre concept is also being explored by other firms - including Google and Blue Origin - and is being pitched as a greener alternative to traditional centres, which require enormous amounts of power to run and water for cooling.

The ever-expanding use of AI computing has seen a massive increase in the amount of data that needs to be stored and processed around the world.

New facilities continue to be built but it is becoming difficult to find places to put them. Data centres are large and sprawling, use enormous amounts of power and water for cooling, and increasingly local people don't want them built nearby.

Data centres were a central issue in the midterm elections earlier in November, with many Democratic candidates promising tougher regulation on their construction.

An expert previously told the BBC that launching hardware into orbit remains expensive and that the infrastructure to protect, cool and power them can be complex - while a growing quantity of space debris puts the physical hardware at risk.

Separately, another cautioned that the growing number of low-orbit craft increases the possibility of crashes between objects - which could damage machines or send materials falling back to earth.

Starlink has been accused of creating congestion in space, something Musk denies. He has also rejected claims his satellites have been taking up too much room and crowding out competitors.

#117. Some U.S. food banks see demand top pandemic levels

During the Covid-19 pandemic, Greg Silverman watched an MTA bus driver pull over on Manhattan's Upper West Side, let his passengers off and run across the street toward him.

Silverman, who runs New York nonprofit West Side Campaign Against Hunger, initially thought the driver wanted to use the bathroom at the church where the food distribution was taking place. Instead, the man asked, "Can I get some food?"

"He was a full-time employee," Silverman said. "You are an essential personnel, making the city survive and you need food."

Now, Silverman said, his organization has seen demand surpass even the levels it observed during that time of crisis when the U.S. economy ground to a halt — and many of the people now seeking food also have jobs. Silverman expects the nonprofit to distribute as much as 6 million pounds of food this year.

"The height of the pandemic was not the height of need, by any means," he said.

The pressure on the emergency food system extends beyond New York. Nationally, use of charitable food programs remains elevated as sustained inflation makes it harder for Americans to afford food, housing and healthcare. Higher prices come at the same time that the federal government has restricted food aid.

The Department of Agriculture's latest annual report found that 13.7% of U.S. households, roughly 18.3 million households, experienced food insecurity in 2024. That rate was significantly higher than in every year from 2016 through 2021.

The pandemic initially brought a surge in demand for charitable food programs, which was followed by government support. In the years that followed, pandemic-era policies such as expanded Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program, or SNAP, benefits, the enhanced child tax credit and school meal programs phased out as inflation spiked.

"This is not because you can't go to work," said City Harvest President Carlos Rodriguez. "Primarily, your work and your wages are just not keeping up with the way costs have gone up in the last five years alone."

City Harvest, which works with food providers across the five boroughs of New York, told CNBC 1 in 5 New Yorkers is currently experiencing food insecurity, or limited access to enough food because of a lack of money or other resources. That is up from 1 in 6 a year earlier, and 1 in 8 before the pandemic.

Average monthly visits to food pantries and soup kitchens in New York are also 85% higher than before the pandemic, said Rodriguez.

In the Federal Reserve's October Beige Book, the central bank's New York regional branch described a sharp rise in first-time food pantry users. One nonprofit leader told the Federal Reserve Bank of New York that the strain was comparable to the pandemic, "but without the same philanthropic and government support."

Data from Plentiful, a technology platform that connects people with food pantries and tracks their usage, also points to growing demand and limited resources.

"For pantries using our reservation and pre-registration features, we see that the overwhelming majority of those are at or over capacity," said Catherine Compitello, CEO of Plentiful. "Demand is growing while supply is not."

First-time users of Plentiful in New York City rose roughly 56% from August to October, Compitello said. It was the second-largest fall surge since 2016; the only larger one came during last year's federal government shutdown, when delayed SNAP benefits drove more people to seek food assistance.

Compitello said that when families have trouble affording essentials, they often prioritize healthcare, transportation, childcare and utilities, leaving food as the expense they cut back on.

Demand for food banks is rising in other regions of the country, and nonprofits are facing their own operational challenges as they try to meet it.

A spokesperson for the Los Angeles Regional Food Bank told CNBC the organization's diesel bill more than doubled in September, to roughly $75,000, adding to the cost of delivering food and running refrigerated trucks.

Demand in the region is also spiking. Distribution in the Los Angeles area for January through September totaled 116 million pounds of food and grocery products — the equivalent of 93 million meals — and slightly higher than a near-record year in 2025, according to the Los Angeles Regional Food Bank.

Federal food aid changes have exacerbated the problems. Rodriguez of City Harvest described SNAP as a first line of defense against hunger, saying the program can provide as many as nine meals for every one meal the organization provides. But City Harvest has estimated at least 8.7% of SNAP recipients in New York City lost benefits over the past year, after congressional Republicans and President Donald Trump approved work certification requirements and changes to eligibility.

The pressure is also mounting in Los Angeles County, where roughly 260,000 CalFresh recipients are subject to expanded federal work requirements that took effect in June. CalFresh is California's version of SNAP.

That does not mean all 260,000 will lose benefits, since people who meet the work requirements or qualify for an exemption can remain eligible. But a Los Angeles Regional Food Bank spokesperson told CNBC the changes add another layer of uncertainty for households already struggling with food costs.

In Atlanta, the pressure extends beyond the city itself to the surrounding region. The Atlanta Community Food Bank serves about 270,000 households a month, or nearly 700,000 people, through partner pantries across North Georgia. President and CEO Kyle Waide said the number of people it serves has jumped more than 70% in five years.

"Much of that increase is among people whose incomes are too high to qualify for federal benefit programs," said Waide.

Under current SNAP guidelines in Georgia, most households must have gross monthly income at or below 130% of the federal poverty level to qualify. They must also meet a separate net income test. For fiscal 2027, which began Oct. 1, the gross income limit is $2,960 a month for a household of three, or about $35,520 annually, and $3,575 a month for a household of four, or $42,900 a year.

More than 60% of Atlanta Community Food Bank clients have household incomes above the cutoff for SNAP eligibility, Waide said, generally in the mid-to-upper $30,000 range depending on household size. The food bank does not collect everyone's income, he said, but the figure is based on survey estimates.

Given those dynamics, Waide said he does not expect the need to ease.

"We're preparing for the level of demand to stay where it is today, or even increase more," Waide said. "We don't see a big source of relief coming anytime soon."

#116. Judge blocks FCC early-renewal process for Disney-owned ABC stations, for now

A judge has temporarily blocked the Federal Communications Commission from continuing its early renewal of a set of broadcast station licenses for Disney-owned ABC.

Judge Loren L. AliKhan of U.S. District Court in Washington granted Disney the preliminary injunction it sought and denied the FCC's attempt to dismiss the suit.

"This is not a close case," wrote the federal judge. "Given the extreme, indeed existential, nature of the possible penalty Plaintiffs face—loss of their broadcast licenses—the court concludes that early license renewal proceedings create a risk sufficient to chill a broadcaster of ordinary firmness."

The broadcast network had filed the lawsuit and followed the FCC beginning its early review of the ABC licenses. The government agency cited concerns around ABC's diversity, equity and inclusion efforts as the reason behind the start of the early renewal — years ahead of their scheduled expiration.

"Today's decision is an important victory for the First Amendment," said an ABC spokesperson.

An FCC spokesperson said in a statement that the part of the decision granting ABC's motion "is wrong on the facts and wrong on the law," the Associated Press reported.

In her ruling, AliKhan said that early license renewal proceedings came over a year into the FCC's enforcement push against ABC, starting with an investigation into Disney's employment practices in March 2025 and leading to the renewal proceedings in April this year.

ABC on Tuesday had said that it continued to "face irreparable harm" from the administration, which "has been acting" through the FCC, which is run by President Donald Trump appointee Brendan Carr.

AliKhan, a Joe Biden appointee, noted in her ruling that ABC had to change how it handled guest appearances for top shows "The View" and "Jimmy Kimmel Live!" with no candidate for public office appearing on the former and a senatorial candidate's appearance on the latter uploaded to YouTube instead of being broadcast.

"The prospect of license non-renewal has also factored into plaintiffs' calculus of when and how they cover President Trump and political topics," said the judge.

ABC had alleged that Trump and Carr have been making comments "intended to chill their protected speech," and that such comments have escalated since the 2024 presidential election.

The White House and the FCC didn't immediately respond to requests for comment from CNBC.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan contributed to the story

#114. US announces new tariffs on host of countries including China

Watch: What we know about Trump's Thanksgiving tariffs

The US has announced a fresh raft of tariffs as President Donald Trump pushes ahead with his signature trade policy.

The new taxes charged on imports to America are set to hit many of the country's biggest trading partners - China, the European Union and 12 other jurisdictions, including Japan, South Korea and Taiwan.

The White House began investigating the trade practices of other countries in March after the US Supreme Court ruled Trump's previously announced Liberation Day tariffs were illegal.

China is facing the highest tariff rate of up to 40%, followed by Vietnam at up to 35%, with others facing either 20% or 25%.

The US Trade Representative (USTR) announced the proposed tariffs together with extensive reports of how each jurisdiction's trade practices purportedly impact American commerce.

The new duties are not expected to take effect until next year and have to go through rounds of public consultation and, potentially, hearings.

Tariffs are taxes levied on imported goods. The amount is typically calculated as a percentage of a good's value and paid to the government by businesses importing products.

Some of these rates will be effectively reduced because of caps on tariffs Trump has agreed to. Individual countries also face different levies.

The proposed tariffs will affect goods from China, the European Union, Singapore, Switzerland, Indonesia, Malaysia, Cambodia, Thailand, Korea, Vietnam, Taiwan, Bangladesh, Japan, and India.

The USTR's Jamieson Greer said the latest tariffs issued would "level the playing field for American workers and chart a new, more sustainable course for the world economy".

He said that many of America's trading partners had "for decades" adopted industrial strategies which included "targeting manufacturing, wage and labor suppression".

They also employ "other market distorting practices that tilt the trade balance in their favour while eroding America's manufacturing base", Greer said.

But economists have warned tariffs can fuel inflation and push prices up for consumers if businesses pass on higher costs, as well as dampen global trade.

Trump has repeatedly threatened or imposed tariffs during his second term, arguing they will boost US manufacturing and create jobs.

Despite a poor result for the Republican party in the midterm elections, the president has continued to press on with his flagship trade policy.

Trump is also seeking to cut the US trade deficit - the gap between the value of goods it buys from other countries and those it sells to them.

Trump's so-called Liberation Day tariffs were struck down by the Supreme Court earlier this year

These new levies are build on a "stronger legal foundation" under US law compared with Trump's April 2025 Liberation Day tariffs, Singapore Management University economics lecturer Chang Pao-Li said.

Trump previously invoked emergency presidential powers to impose tariffs that have since been struck down, while his latest approach relies on the USTR's authority to impose tariffs to address certain foreign trade practices, she said.

The levies are still likely to face legal challenges, but there has been a "historical precedent" for such tariffs remaining in force, Chang said.

Many tariffs imposed under Section 301 - a law that allows the US to probe and retaliate against foreign trade practices - during Trump's first term remained in effect during his successor Joe Biden's administration.

However, Stephen Brown, chief North America economist for Capital Economics, said the latest tariff announcement was "not as alarming as it looks", due to the taxes "essentially replacing" previously announced tariffs made in the summer.

"The increase in the effective tariff rate looks set to be a touch lower than we've been assuming for our forecasts, although the new tariffs will still provide a marginal boost to prices," he said in a note.

The additional tariff burden is likely to be small because a limited number of goods are subject to the new duties, Brown added.

Jurisdictions affected by the new wave of tariffs have voiced their disapproval over the Trump administration's move.

Washington has also proposed a 10% tariff on goods from Brazil, as part of a separate investigation over what it says are "harmful trade practices" undercutting American workers.

Brazilian authorities expressed their "outrage" at the announcement, saying that the move is aimed at pressuring the government to change regulations around issues like digital trade.

The South American nation faces duties on finished aircraft and sugar sold to the US.

"Brazil will not yield to such threats," President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva's office said.

The European Commission rejected the new US tariffs, saying it would investigate the reasons for bringing them in. It pointed out that tariffs are limited to the 15% previously agreed.

The latest tariff rate is "not consistent" with the reality of bilateral economic-trade cooperation between the US and Vietnam, Vietnamese Deputy Prime Minister Bui Thanh Son said.

The Japanese government said the US tariffs were "regrettable", while the Singapore government said it disagrees with the White House's findings and that it is engaging the USTR to explore ways to reduce the tariff.

South Korea's trade minister said the proposed 15% tariff will be "more than a decent outcome" as it would fall in line with a previously agreed limit with Washington.

Last year, Seoul pledged $350bn (Â£267bn) in US investments in exchange for lower tariffs as part of a trade deal.

Trump tariffs on Asia: Which economies were hit hardest?

China's growth accelerates as AI boom helps boost exports

US trading partners have been "waiting for this shoe to drop" and have argued that they are being wrongly accused of producing too much, said the Asia Society Policy Institute's Wendy Cutler, a former senior US trade negotiator.

"Many believe that China, not them, should be the target for US action," she added.

Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun described the new tariffs as a form of "political manipulation", describing as "false" the US claim that China produces more than it can consume.

The tariff announcement is likely to increase tensions between the US and China, the world's two biggest economies, who remain locked in a trade dispute and the AI race.

The two countries have also been embroiled in a tit-for-tat tariffs war, which is currently on hold.

Despite last week's meeting between Trump and China's President Xi Jinping, the two sides made no announcement on the trade war truce, which is set to expire on 10 January.

Beijing has remained vague on how it will respond but for now seems "prepared to live with the new rates provided they don't exceed 20%", Cutler said.

Additional reporting by Leehyun Choi in Seoul

#112. What's gone wrong at Poundland?

Watch: Why has Poundland collapsed into administration?

The future of Poundland hangs in the balance after the discount retailer's collapse into administration.

Its owner, US investment firm Gordon Brothers, put it up for sale in September, but then rejected offers to keep the business afloat.

Poundland has a major presence on the High Street, with 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, where it trades under the Dealz brand.

Its collapse puts the jobs of its 11,000 employees at risk, as well as potentially thousands of others at firms that supply the retailer.

People have not stopped shopping at Poundland - quite the opposite.

Although the chain was struggling when it was sold by Pepco last year for Â£1, the business turned a corner over the year to September, going from a Â£68m loss to an Â£18.1m profit before interest, tax and certain accounting charges.

Going into the vital Christmas period, first-quarter like-for-like sales were up 7% and operational profits of Â£1.9m were up Â£1.2m on last year.

According to Julie Palmer, managing partner at insolvency specialist Begbies Traynor, "this isn't a story about shoppers abandoning Poundland".

She told the BBC the tills were "busier than they were a year ago", adding that on the face of it a turnaround seemed "well under way".

However, Gordon Brothers had been restricting Poundland's access to cash needed to restock stores and meet day-to-day expenses.

Poundland faced "a funding and ownership problem rather than a trading collapse", according to Palmer.

However, while Poundland boss Barry Williams claimed the business was making a "strong recovery", Gordon Brothers painted a different picture, saying the chain remained "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It said it had provided significant funding to support the company's ability to trade since it acquired the business.

Poundland collapses putting 11,000 jobs at risk

Poundland workers 'in limbo' over firm's future

It has also made about Â£18m from a combination of debt interest payments and other fees and charges.

As well as being Poundland's owner, it is one of its lenders, and is owed loans, including a Â£22.5m shareholder loan it inherited when it bought the business for Â£1.

As the main creditor, Gordon Brothers would be first in line to get paid back if the business goes under - but its suppliers are not.

"When the owner is also the main lender, its first priority is recovering [loans]," says Palmer. "Sometimes the route that best protects the lender is different from the one that saves the most jobs, and that tension is why this has become such a political story."

Irish firm Martin Property Group, London-based holding company Xyra Group and German investment firm Zachert Private Equity are all also understood to have made offers for Poundland.

Gordon Brothers had also been weighing options to break Poundland up.

Its view was that ultimately breaking the retailer up would provide more security for employees in the long term, because any buyers of its store estate would also have to agree to keep the employees on.

Insiders who are critical of how Gordon Brothers has handled the Poundland sale accuse it of driving the business into the ground to make money through the insolvency process.

But the investment firm, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied the claim.

Now that the company has entered administration, consultants at Alix Partners have been appointed to pursue a sale of the business and its assets.

There are various options on the table, which include securing a buyer for the business either in whole or piecemeal.

The prime minister has said he wants "every effort made to secure a deal that protects jobs and keeps as many shops open as possible".

In the immediate future, Poundland stores and distribution centres will continue operating as normal.

#110. Vance says he was assured Jensen Huang was not under DOJ investigation

Vice President JD Vance said Wednesday that Attorney General Todd Blanche told him Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang was not under investigation.

"I was told by the Department of Justice and multiple officials in our national security apparatus that he was under no investigation," Vance told reporters in Ohio on Wednesday after being asked what assurances he had gotten that Huang was not under investigation. "Not even that he was about to be prosecuted; that he's under no investigation."

Vance spoke two days after President Donald Trump told CNBC in an interview that an investigation had been done.

Trump was asked what Blanche had told him about potential criminal liability for Huang and the question of whether Nvidia chips had ended up in China in violation of U.S. export laws.

"They did a pretty good investigation, and they felt that he, himself, was exonerated," Trump said Monday.

Reuters reported Wednesday that the Department of Justice did not conduct a formal investigation into Huang before Trump pushed for his reinstatement as CEO.

Blanche had concluded there was no evidence Huang had broken any laws based on investigations into Nvidia clients Super Micro Computer and Megaspeed, according to the report.

The DOJ did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the Reuters report. And the White House didn't immediately respond to a request for comment on Vance saying Huang was under no investigation when Trump had said there was one. Nvidia didn't immediately respond to a request for comment about Vance's remarks.

Blanche on Tuesday dodged questions about whether there had been an investigation.

"What the president said is that he talked to a lot of people going into the decision that he made to encourage the board to reinstate him," Blanche said after being asked whether the DOJ had investigated Huang and, if so, what were the results of the probe.

Huang was reinstated as CEO of Nvidia on Monday, after the chipmaker's board of directors said it had received "input from senior levels of the United States government" that Huang was not a national security risk. Trump took credit for Huang's reinstatement in the interview with CNBC shortly after Nvidia's announcement.

Huang resigned following The New York Times reporting multiple affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who is alleged to be a spy for Beijing and who was an executive for the Singapore-based cloud provider Megaspeed.

The reporting added fuel to allegations that Nvidia should have known its artificial intelligence chips were being diverted to China and being used by Chinese companies, violating U.S. export laws.

"The product they create is a very important part of us staying ahead of the SI arms race with China. We really do care about ensuring those chips do not fall into the wrong hands," Vance said, using the Trump administration's preferred acronym for AI, which the president calls "super intelligence."

"We actually see this is an opportunity for Nvidia to focus on complying with American export controls, and we think Jensen Huang is going to take that very seriously," Vance continued.

"We received the assurances that he was not under any significant — he was under no investigation — and the accusations against him were based on anonymous reporting, not on actual evidence," Vance said.

On Tuesday, Trump left the door open to pardon Huang in an interview with the New York Post, but Vance said on Wednesday that the CEO doesn't need one.

"I don't think he needs a pardon at all," Vance said. "I haven't seen any evidence again that he committed a crime. And if there is evidence that he committed a crime, then that's up to relevant authorities to actually bring a prosecution against him."

Huang is scheduled to appear for a closed-door interview with House committees about his dealings with Megaspeed on Tuesday. At the same time, Nvidia will hold its annual conference where it showcases new technology in Washington.

— Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#108. Poundland collapses putting 11,000 jobs at risk

Watch: Why has Poundland collapsed into administration?

Poundland has collapsed into administration, putting 11,000 jobs at risk, after its owners failed to find a buyer to keep it in business.

The retailer's shops and distribution centres will remain open and continue to trade during the administration process with the business likely to be broken up and some of its stores sold off to rival retailers.

Poundland's US owners held talks with several bidders over a last-minute rescue deal, but no agreement was reached.

Poundland boss Barry Williams said in a note to staff that the board had "no alternative but to take this decision", but was making it with "sincere sadness and intense regret".

He said the US owners Gordon Brothers had removed "all borrowing facilities and access to our bank accounts" on 27 October, and adequate funds had not been released since then to keep the business solvent.

"Under these circumstances, it is impossible for the board of directors to see how Poundland can continue to operate as a going concern," he added.

Kelvin Blake, pictured at a protest in Walsall, has worked for Poundland for 25 years

Kelvin Blake, 62, who has worked for the firm for 25 years, and whose partner has been there 29 years, said he was feeling "down, depressed", and because of his age, feared he would not find a job elsewhere.

"We didn't want it, but it's come so now we've just got to face it and see what the future holds.

"My income's gone," he added. "Obviously, we'll be working until we're told to leave."

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said Poundland was "part of the fabric of high streets and communities up and down the country".

He said the news of it going into administration just weeks before Christmas would be "deeply concerning for workers and their families".

He added: "While it is worrying, going into administration does give other potential buyers the opportunity to engage and we will work hard with all the relevant parties to ensure the best possible outcome."

Alix Partners, which has been appointed as administrator, said they would look at all options for the business, "including sales of the business and its assets, either in whole or part".

"We appreciate this is a deeply concerning time for all involved - customers, suppliers and, in particular, Poundland colleagues," they said.

Gordon Brothers has been criticised over how it handled a potential sale of Poundland, with accusations the investment firm hoped to profit from selling off the chain's 600 shops and stock piecemeal after running it into the ground.

Gordon Brothers, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied such claims.

On the funding question, Gordon Brothers said it had loaned the firm Â£85m over a year to help save it from insolvency.

But insiders critical of the US firm have said it stands to make its loans back and more from the insolvency process.

Previously, Gordon Brothers netted millions from liquidating the stock of fashion retailer LK Bennett, and in insolvency fees.

Labour MP Liam Byrne told the BBC the House of Commons' Business, Innovation, Science and Trade Committee will recall Gordon Brothers before Parliament - two weeks after Gordon Brothers representatives last appeared at the committee to answer questions about Poundland, and where MPs said they were "deeply concerned" about the business.

Byrne said: "I am really worried that a 'greed is good' approach to business by its owners has forced [Poundland] into an administration."

He added the committee wants to know "how much money they're ripping out and crucially, how do we make sure, as a country, that we don't have this kind of scenario unfold again".

Gordon Brothers met Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds on Monday to update him on the progress.

The firm said on Wednesday that despite the Poundland management bringing about a turnaround over the past year, the firm was "still vulnerable" to inflation and any downturn in sales or profit margin, and it had faced "several years of instability".

A spokesperson added the firm's "thoughts are with Poundland colleagues".

It said it had stepped in to take over the retailer in 2025 when it was struggling, and when "no-one else was willing to finance the business".

One of the central stumbling blocks in negotiations to keep the business afloat was a shareholder loan Gordon Brothers inherited when it bought the business for less than Â£1 last year.

Gordon Brothers was owed Â£22.5m of that loan, while the previous owner Pepco was owed Â£7.5m.

Gordon Brothers rejected various offers to buy the business as a going concern with deals to pay off that loan.

Irish firm Martin Property Group offered to pay off Â£5m of the loan up front, and defer the rest of the payments to 2030.

London-based Xyra group offered Â£12.5m up front but ran out of time to get a deal over the line.

German investment firm Zachert Private Equity is also understood to have made a late offer.

The crisis at Poundland really came to a head over the past few weeks after Gordon Brothers restricted access to funds for day-to-day operations.

This meant that stock at the retailer was not being replenished at the necessary pace in the vital run-up to the festive period.

#105. New UK-EU treaties have 10-year termination clause, BBC understands

The government and the EU are set to publish the texts of two treaties that were agreed last week as part of a post-Brexit reset.

The treaties will be published on Thursday afternoon, alongside legislation which is designed to enact the bulk of the deal.

The BBC understands the treaties have a 10-year termination clause that would mean if one party ended the agreement, they would have to pay to rebuild border checks infrastructure, which will be significantly scaled back as part of the new deal.

Opposition leader Kemi Badenoch and Reform UK's Nigel Farage have suggested they would refuse to pay.

The Brexit reset deal has already sparked debate about its benefits, its costs, and a wider discussion on the long-term UK-EU relationship.

The deal covers British food exports, emissions trading and a youth experience scheme.

Two of the three legal texts will be released on Thursday, covering the food trade and emissions trading scheme deals, alongside the government's cost-cutting bill to enact the deal.

New assessments of the impact of the deal on different sectors and regions of the country are also expected.

EU offers UK fresh deal on 'Made in Europe' scheme

Badenoch challenges Burnham over university fees for EU students

Farage helps launch new campaign against the UK rejoining the EU

Under the deal, food exporters will be significant beneficiaries from the scrapping of considerable red tape and checks, and dismantling of border facilities. An internal analysis forecasts a 16% boost to agricultural exports.

The industry and government hope the reduction in costs for food producers and sellers will be passed on to customers, but there may be no clear visible impact at the checkout.

Privately, industry insiders told the BBC earlier this week that there are too many moving parts in the world economy, in particular the energy price shock resulting from war in the Middle East.

Regarding the fine detail in the text of one of the treaties relating to the 10-year termination clause, European affairs minister Hamish Falconer said: "The Cutting Costs Bill allows us to implement two major trade agreements."

"It allows us to eliminate paperwork for our food and drink traders, protect billions of pounds worth of British exports from carbon taxes, and make doing business with our largest trading partner cheaper and easier."

The BBC understands that a plan to link British and European emissions trading schemes will see more coverage across the UK's aviation and maritime industries.

The treaty text will also contain finer detail of how the UK will observe, shape but not vote on relevant EU rules.

Plans to use secondary legislation, including granting direct so-called "Henry VIII powers" to ministers to align UK and EU laws, will also be detailed in the legislation.

Such powers could enable ministers to make changes to bills with less Parliamentary scrutiny.

Outlining the agreement with the EU to MPs earlier this week, the prime minister said new agreements with the EU are "pragmatic" and "not a prelude to anything".

Andy Burnham also said it would be "unthinkable" for the UK to rejoin the bloc without a referendum.

Several former prime ministers have urged him not to reopen the Brexit debate. Burnham said they are "stuck in the past" and later claimed there would be "no banging on about Europe" from Labour.

Badenoch said Burnham has "oversold" the deal and was "trying to take Britain back to 2015".

#104. Best Buy reports comparable sales growth of 6.1%, highest in five years

Best Buy on Tuesday beat Wall Street expectations on the top and bottom lines for its third fiscal quarter and raised its full-year guidance.

The consumer electronics retailer reported comparable sales growth of 6.1%, a significant beat over expectations of 2.3%, according to market intelligence platform StreetAccount. The company said that growth was its highest in five years. It reported a domestic comparable sales increase of 6.2% and international comparable sales growth of 4.9%.

"We have a tremendous amount of momentum," CEO Jason Bonfig told CNBC. "The fact that we saw pretty much broad-based growth across almost all of our categories is a lot of momentum that we're very excited about."

Best Buy also raised its guidance for the rest of the year. It's now expecting full-year revenue of between $43 billion and $43.3 billion compared with a previous expectation of between $42.3 billion and $42.8 billion. It's expecting comparable sales to be up 3.4% to 4% compared with prior guidance of between 1.9% and 3%.

The earnings report is the company's first since Bonfig took over as CEO from Corie Barry at the beginning of the month.

Bonfig said the retailer saw overperformance in both computing and gaming for the quarter, driven by recent game launches and a targeted product assortment.

Looking ahead into the holiday season, he said the Best Buy consumer remains "resilient," but that they are focused on getting value wherever they can.

"We did see not only our current customers continue to be engaged, but we actually did see more new customers come into the brand, as well as we had some customers that we re-engaged as well," Bonfig said. "So those are really, really positive signs for us in Q3, and all of that was factored into the way that we guided for Q4 and the rest of the year."

Here's how the company performed in the period ended Oct. 31 compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Best Buy reported net income of $353 million, or $1.66 per share, compared with $140 million, or 66 cents per share, the year prior. Adjusting for one-time items, including restructuring charges, Best Buy posted adjusted earnings of $1.62 per share.

Revenue rose to $10.24 billion from $9.67 billion in the year-ago period. Best Buy reported domestic revenue of $9.42 billion and international revenue of $820 million.

Tuesday's earnings are the latest in a series of strong reports from the retailer, which has been instituting a turnaround strategy to return to growth after getting hit by declining foot traffic, lower consumer sentiment, tariffs and memory costs.

Shares of Best Buy are up nearly 45% this year.

Bonfig told CNBC last quarter that the launch of "Grand Theft Auto VI" in the current quarter is expected to drive sales in the gaming category, which has been seeing gains over the past year. Last month, Best Buy launched a new gaming experience for customers featuring gaming content, technology and special offers, among other features.

"I think there'll still be opportunities for gaming to continue to drive our business moving forward," Bonfig told CNBC on Tuesday.

#101. U.S. proposes new tariffs on 14 trading partners, including China and the EU

The U.S. is preparing new tariffs on 14 economies, including China, the EU, Japan and India, following investigations into what the Trump administration says are excess manufacturing practices that disadvantage U.S. commerce.

The administration launched those and other trade investigations in March, weeks after the Supreme Court unraveled a major piece of President Donald Trump's tariff-heavy economic policy by striking down his "liberation day" duties. Trump has since been accused by critics of his tariffs of trying to revive the canceled duties using other legal authorities.

The latest action targets foreign economies that create, sustain or contribute to structural excess capacity and production in manufacturing sectors that the U.S. finds unreasonable and burdensome or restrictive to its commerce, a senior U.S. official told reporters Tuesday evening.

The new tariffs are "targeted," not blanket rates, and will vary for each economy, and many will not exceed the rates established in previously reached trade deals, said the official, who spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss the forthcoming duties.

The rates, which mostly range from 15% to 20%, are "not really additive" and generally will not stack on top of existing duties, U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC's "Squawk Box" Wednesday morning.

"In effect, it'll look more like a top up," Greer said. "When it all nets out, it really will just top up a few percentage points."

An exception is China, which faces a 20% rate under the new tariff policy, "so it will go up some," Greer said. But at the same time, both countries are moving to remove tariffs on some of each other's goods in what Greer called "stability-generating activity through this action."

The senior U.S. official said Tuesday that the 20% rate will come on top of the so-called forced technology transfer-related tariffs of roughly 25%, which were implemented during Trump's first presidential term. U.S. tariffs on Chinese goods will therefore reach about 45%, give or take for different goods, the official said.

Average U.S. tariffs rates on Chinese goods stood at about 36.5% as of July, according to the Congressional Research Service.

Greer also said on CNBC that the new policy likely will not take effect for months.

"We've talked to our trading partners about this. They know what we're proposing," he said. "I think a lot of this is baked in, and I don't think any of these would actually take effect earlier than a couple months from now."

The administration says the tariffs target what it calls "overcapacity" — excess industrial production, often supported by government subsidies, that allows cheaper goods to flood global markets and undercut U.S. producers.

China has disputed the allegations. "The so-called 'overcapacity' does not really exist," China's foreign ministry said Wednesday before the official reports were released by the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative, according to Reuters.

The overcapacity levies are being imposed under Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974. The Trump administration invoked the same statute to launch investigations into 60 economies over alleged forced-labor issues.

Those investigations, which also began in March, led to broad tariffs of 10% to 12.5% on goods from dozens of countries covering 99.4% of U.S. imports.

The Section 301 actions came weeks after the Supreme Court struck down the near-global tariffs that Trump had enacted under the International Emergency Economic Powers Act.

Shortly after the court's ruling, Trump invoked Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974 to impose a 10% global tariff. That tariff expired after 150 days, and was immediately replaced with the forced-labor-related duties.

An ongoing lawsuit accuses the Trump administration of citing forced-labor issues as a "pretext" in pursuit of reviving Trump's worldwide tariff regime.

#98. Retail turnarounds are taking hold just in time for the holiday season

Once-struggling retailers are entering the holiday quarter with improved performance and sales momentum as turnaround efforts begin to resonate with shoppers.

A handful of earnings reports on Tuesday pointed to a mix of factors behind the strong results, including a resilient consumer, stronger execution and demand for the right products at the right price.

Kohl's reported comparable sales growth of 1.2% for the period ended Oct. 31 and raised its full-year outlook as CEO Michael Bender said customers "have remained resilient" but are "migrating to value with their purchases."

He tied improvement to the company's work on product assortment, store experience and value.

Best Buy also on Tuesday posted its highest comparable sales growth in five years, at 6.1%, which prompted the electronics retailer to raise its full-year outlook.

"We have a tremendous amount of momentum," CEO Jason Bonfig told CNBC in an interview. "The fact that we saw pretty much broad-based growth across almost all of our categories is a lot of momentum that we're very excited about."

The company also credited execution, market share gains and a compelling product cycle alongside growing customer demand.

At Dick's Sporting Goods, quarterly comparable sales rose 2.6% at the core business and 0.5% at Foot Locker. CEO Lauren Hobart said "all income demographics grew" and the company "didn't see trade-down from best to better or better to good."

She similarly pointed to new products and market share gains within the category as contributing to the strong quarter. She also highlighted continued improvement at Foot Locker.

All three companies have been undergoing efforts to boost sales and get the right products on shelves to meet customer demand.

Target, likewise undergoing revitalization efforts, last week reported comparable sales growth of 5.6% in the third quarter, fueled by a 5% increase in comparable traffic. The retailer raised its full-year outlook.

CEO Michael Fiddelke at the time called the traffic gain "a healthy sign that guests are responding to our changes and choosing Target more often."

Even Gap CEO Richard Dickson told CNBC the company has moved from "rebuild mode" to "turnaround mode" at its Old Navy banner, a sign of progress, albeit not a broad-based portfolio recovery.

Michael Baker, a retail analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC he sees both a resilient consumer and company-specific improvements as driving sector strength.

"Retail sales have been more resilient than most think," Baker said. His analysis showed retail sales, excluding autos and gas, rose 5.1% in the third quarter on a nonseasonally adjusted basis — the strongest quarter since the pandemic.

But he cautioned that growth appears to be coming more from transaction size than traffic, with inflation, promotions and higher units per transaction driving up spend.

Dollar Tree, which reported better-than-expected earnings on Tuesday morning, posted a 3.8% increase to traffic, accelerating from 0.4% growth in the prior quarter, suggesting more shoppers may be bargain hunting. Comparable sales at the retailer increased 4.6% during the period.

"Consumers are under pressure, and they are increasingly selective about where and how they spend," said Dollar Tree CEO Michael Creedon on the company's earnings call. "When under pressure, shoppers prioritize value and buy closer to need."

Comparable sales of everyday essentials rose 8.9% at Dollar Tree stores in the third quarter, while sales of nonessential items were essentially flat, the company said.

— CNBC's Laya Neelakandan contributed to this report.

#96. SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts

SpaceX said Wednesday it will donate one share of the company's class A stock to every active Trump Account, and CEO Elon Musk would personally match the gift.

"SpaceX is building the infrastructure of the future and working to make life multiplanetary," the company said in a statement. "We believe ownership of the company creating these systems should extend to the next generation of Americans for their long-term benefit."

The White House announced in October that nearly 70 million Trump Accounts had been created, including more than 60 million through auto-enrollment. It's unclear how many are considered active.

Musk said in a social media post on X on Thursday that the share from SpaceX would be transferred next month, without providing further details. "The share directly from me will be transferred when my lockup expires in June next year," he added.

Eligible children must have their account claimed to enable contributions from family members, friends and employers, and to receive the government's one-time $1,000 seed contribution. Auto-enrolled children can receive philanthropic contributions without a parent or guardian claiming an account, according to TrumpAccounts.gov.

Representatives for SpaceX and the U.S. Department of the Treasury did not immediately respond to a request for further details about the planned gift.

SpaceX Chief Operating Officer Gwynne Shotwell had previously agreed to make a personal gift of over 2 million SpaceX shares to Trump Accounts, with eligible account holders expected to receive them in December.

In October, Elon Musk posted on X that he would give a share of SpaceX to every kid in America next year.

Trump Accounts, also known as 530A accounts, are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Parents, guardians, grandparents and others can collectively contribute up to $5,000 per child per year in after-tax dollars up until the year before the beneficiary turns 18. The annual contribution limit is indexed for inflation after 2027.

Since the July 4 launch, families and friends have collectively contributed more than $1 billion to Trump Accounts. Total deposits now sit at more than $9 billion, CNBC first reported.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the Treasury. Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria.

The Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold stock donations, a move officials have said is meant to encourage more philanthropic gifts.

Contributing stock can benefit donors, who are able to avoid capital gains taxes triggered by selling appreciated stock. But families can face restrictions related to selling that donated stock. The policy also means some children's wealth accumulation could be tied to the performance of individual companies.

Under U.S. Department of the Treasury guidelines, families cannot refuse stock gifted to Trump Accounts and generally cannot sell it until the earlier of five years or the end of the calendar year in which the beneficiary turns 17.

SpaceX shares have been under some pricing pressure as the company negotiates a possible merger with Elon Musk's automaker Tesla. The companies have established two special committees to handle merger negotiations, and hired advisers for a potential mega-deal.

CNBC's Sam Meredith, Kevin Breuninger, Kate Dore and Jess Dickler contributed to this story

#93. Secondhand shopping is going mainstream this holiday season as consumers seek value

As the high cost of living squeezes consumers' wallets this holiday season, more shoppers are turning to secondhand retail and thrifting.

The shift comes as consumers look for better value, buyers put more emphasis on personalization and younger generations change the way they shop. The U.S. secondhand apparel market grew 13% in 2025, nearly four times faster than the overall retail clothing market, according to GlobalData.

That segment is expected to reach nearly $80 billion by 2030, growing more than 7% annually on average, GlobalData found. Much of that expansion — 40% — was driven by Generation Z, followed closely by millennials.

"I think this has been their version of the thrill of the hunt that millennials loved during the recession days," Katherine Cullen, a global senior retail analyst at EY, told CNBC. "It's a way to really express an individual style, find something unique, something that really fits their own vibe and personality."

It goes beyond people shopping for themselves, Cullen said. With more emphasis on unique holiday gifts, shoppers are leaning more on secondhand retail than they have before, whether in clothing or in other retail categories.

They're looking for something that fits the personality of the person they're shopping for, she added.

For Vanessa Roberts, that meant finding two antique children's chairs that she says will perfectly round out her nieces' play space. Roberts, 37, said she's been thrifting for at least two decades, each time embracing the "thrill" of finding something unique or vintage.

Especially in fashion, at a time when the quality of mass-produced items has decreased, thrifted items offer more value beyond just the price tag, Roberts added.

"It's crazy that you can find better quality items at the thrift store right now from 20 years ago versus what you can find at stores right now," she said.

Roberts, who lives in Los Angeles, said she began shopping for holiday gifts in September. As she prepares her haul for December, roughly 70% of her gifting will come from secondhand stores, she estimated.

"It's been a fun little challenge, where it's like a little scavenger hunt when I go into the thrift store," she said. "I'm definitely thrifting for the holidays – I do it every year."

The trend coincides with an economy that has forced consumers to be more intentional about how they spend, as they face high inflation and elevated gas prices.

In October, the consumer price index rose a seasonally adjusted 0.4%, which was more than anticipated. Still, consumers are expected to continue spending through the holiday season, with sales projected to top $1 trillion for the first time — though some of that estimated increase is boosted by inflation.

"You have a consumer who, while they're willing to spend at the top level, they're willing to splurge selectively," Cullen said. "Consumers are looking for value, which I think is something that is making secondhand resale and re-commerce more attractive."

And while clothing is largely the entry point to buying resale, secondhand shopping is increasingly expanding into furniture, electronics and books, according to a report from consumer insights firm The Consumer Collective.

Its October report found that 34% of people surveyed said secondhand products offer better value than brand new ones.

Many secondhand items also have an inherent added value beyond the low price on the tag, according to eBay CEO Jamie Iannone. The uniqueness of those products, as well as the allure that vintage adds to them, means consumers may actually be seeking the benefits of secondhand in more ways than one.

"I can buy a product that's used, and it almost has cachet around it," Iannone told CNBC. "Consumers today, they're able to get these amazing values on products, and it's seen as very normal to be gifting pre-loved."

According to eBay's Recommerce Report in October, 70% of global consumers surveyed said they consider secondhand shopping important for managing their budgets. That same report also found that 89% of respondents plan to spend the same amount or more on secondhand products next year compared with this year.

Iannone said he thinks the interest in secondhand this holiday season is "much stronger" than last year.

"It's becoming just a more established part of consumers' shopping and gifting routines," he said.

At a time when algorithms are driving shopping trends, consumers are also looking for ways to distinguish themselves and their purchases.

That's stretching beyond buying for themselves to purchasing holiday gifts for others, too.

"Secondhand is where they're finding unique items to wear," Alon Rotem, chief strategy officer at ThredUp, told CNBC. The company's holiday survey found that respondents valued uniqueness nearly as much as the value of a thrifted piece.

"To me, that represents a pretty meaningful shift in consumer taste," he added.

It's partly why ThredUp, which is an online thrift marketplace, emphasizes that each of its products is "a snowflake," Rotem said — something unique.

That's the appeal of buying preowned, rather than purchasing off the rack that hundreds of other people are reaching for, too.

"Consumer tastes have changed, and they're much more into vintage," he added. "Uniqueness has a premium in their minds."

Larger retailers are taking note, EY's Cullen said. More retailers, such as Lululemon, Patagonia and Madewell, are dabbling in preowned.

Wall Street is also increasingly watching the growing secondhand trend. Though shares of some secondhand and resale companies such as eBay and Savers Value Village have climbed this year, shares of both ThredUp and The RealReal have tumbled year to date.

"There's a lot of conversation around authenticity or the best find," Cullen said. "I think that interest and momentum, along with some of the economic factors that we're all facing, the idea that people are looking to be frugal is going to just continue."

#90. South Korea's Kospi leads gains as tech rebound lifts Asia markets

Asia-Pacific markets rose Thursday, tracking Wall Street gains overnight, with tech stocks rebounding from steep losses in the prior session.

Investors appear to have looked past a fresh set of U.S. tariffs unveiled by the White House on Wednesday, targeting more than a dozen major trading partners, including China and the European Union.

South Korea's Kospi gained 1.73%, while the small-cap Kosdaq index advanced 2.32% after the country's central bank hiked benchmark lending rates by an expected 25 basis points, as inflation continues to run above target while a booming economy allows it room to tighten policy.

The central bank raised rates to 3.25%, a 2-year high, in its final meeting of the year, data on the Bank of Korea's website showed.

Japan's Nikkei 225 rose 1.21% while the Topix edged 0.71% higher. Australia's S&P/ASX 200 was up 0.15%. Hong Kong's Hang Seng index rose 1.46% while mainland China's CSI 300 added 0.12%.

Asian technology stocks rebounded sharply Thursday, led by Japanese chip-related companies.

SoftBank Group surged more than 11%, while chip-testing equipment maker Advantest rose 7.3%. Lasertec and Disco both gained more than 6%, while Tokyo Electron climbed 4.6%.

In South Korea, memory chip giants SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics advanced more than 4% and 2.1%, respectively. The gains followed a bruising session Wednesday amid renewed concerns over the sustainability of artificial intelligence spending.

Cryptocurrencies also traded higher, with bitcoin rising 1.58% to $60,944.60, while ether gained 1.15% to $1,702.96.

The gains come amid lingering concerns over the outlook for digital assets, with the co-founder of now-shuttered crypto exchange BitMEX Arthur Hayes telling CNBC on Tuesday that bitcoin could test $50,000 if the Nasdaq continues to slide.

Meanwhile, fresh U.S. inflation data reinforced expectations of further monetary tightening by the Federal Reserve. The personal consumption expenditures price index rose 3.8% year on year in October, accelerating from 3.7% in September, while core inflation came in slightly above expectations at 3.3%.

Overnight in the U.S., the Dow Jones Industrial Average erased an 1,100-point plunge on Wednesday, advancing 164.25 points, or 0.33%, to end at 50,182.95. The Nasdaq Composite, however, fell 0.62% to 23,585.80, while the S&P 500 shed 0.25% to close at 7,182.78.

U.S. markets will be closed Thursday for the Thanksgiving holiday and will have a shortened trading session on Friday.

#88. Macy's postpones Thanksgiving Day Parade due to severe weather

Macy's on Wednesday announced it's postponing its Thanksgiving Day Parade, originally scheduled for Thursday, due to severe weather.

The parade, held each year on Thanksgiving morning, will now take place on Saturday at 8:30 a.m. ET, according to the company. The New York City area is expected to have heavy rain and thunderstorms on Thanksgiving.

"The safety of our participants and spectators is our top priority, and after close consultation with our city and agency partners, we have made the decision to move this year's celebration," Macy's said in a statement.

This year's event marks the 100th Macy's Thanksgiving Day Parade. The annual event has never been canceled due to weather, though it was suspended between 1942 and 1944 for World War II.

The parade's viability this year had been in question after concerns arose over strong winds that could become dangerous for the massive balloons that the event is known for. Many of the balloons had already been inflated as of Wednesday afternoon.

The postponement also means thousands of volunteers and artists slated to perform will have to adapt to Saturday's schedule.
